This addendum contains the following seven parts:

b) No-objection letter issued by the national designated authority(ies) or focal person(s).
c) Environmental and social report(s) disclosure;
d) Socioeconomic assessment;
e) Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment;
f) Response from the accredited entity to the independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment; and
g) Gender documentation.

## Note to Accredited Entities on the use of the funding proposal template

• Accredited Entities should provide summary information in the proposal with cross-reference to annexes such as feasibility studies, gender action plan, term sheet, etc.
• Accreditations are valid for 3 years from date of issue. Any changes to the details provided in the funding proposal. Updates to the funding proposal and/or annexes must be reflected in the next accreditation application.
• The total number of pages for the funding proposal (excluding annexes) should not exceed 20 pages. Proposals exceeding the prescribed length will not be assessed within the usual service standard time.
• The recommended font is Arial, size 11.

## Uploading the Funding Proposal

* Project and programme proposals will be disclosed on the GCF website simultaneously with the submission to the Board, subject to confidentiality requirements. Please refer to section G.4 of the GCF Policy for further information regarding disclosure by IDP Accredited Entities as applied to all information on disclosure in section G.4.

fundingproposal@unfccc.int

## A.20 Executing Entity information

The Government of Colombia through the Ministry of Finance and Public Credit (MFPCH) will be the executing entity for this project/programme. The MFPCH is responsible for the GCF as well as the loan resources from CAF. For its part, the Ministry of Agriculture and Rural Development (MADR) will be in charge of the technical implementation of the project through its Directorate of Innovation, Technology and Productivity (DITP). The DITP will also be responsible for the overall technical implementation of the project under the leadership of the MADR, as executing entity (EE). MADR will also be responsible for the supervision of the project’s results. The Ministry of Environment and Sustainable Development (MADS) is in charge of supervising the International Center for Tropical Agriculture (CIAT), as Procurement Party (PP).

The MFPCH will provide technical support to CIAT, which will be responsible for the achievement of project results. The MADR will receive technical support from the Agricultural Research Institute (CENIA) and the National Agricultural Extension Agency (ANECA) in relation to the implementation of the project. The DITP will also receive technical support from the GCF, AGRAFICO (the agricultural research institute in Colombia), CENIA, ANECA, INNOVACOOP, ASOCOAGROCOL, ASOCOAGROINDUSTRIAL, ASOCOAGROINDUSTRIAL (palm oil), ASOCOAQUA-CENCINARA (sugar cane), ASOCOAGROINDUSTRIAL (cocoa), ASOCOAGROINDUSTRIAL (banana), ASOFEDERAZ (rice), FENAFEDE (corn) and FEDEDAN (beefsteak); following the framework agreement signed under Colombian legislation.

## A.21 Executive summary (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

### 1. The climate problem

Colombia is projected to be severely impacted by climate change, with temperature rises of up to +3°C in some regions and a significant increase in the frequency and intensity of extreme weather events. These changes are expected to have severe impacts on agriculture, water resources, and human health.

### 2. Agricultural sector vulnerability

The agricultural sector is particularly vulnerable due to its dependence on climate-sensitive crops such as coffee, bananas, and cocoa. Climate change is projected to lead to increased variability in rainfall patterns, with more frequent and intense droughts and floods. This will have significant impacts on crop yields and the livelihoods of farmers.

### 3. Mitigation and adaptation strategies

To address these challenges, Colombia has developed a range of mitigation and adaptation strategies. These include improving water management practices, developing climate-resilient crops, and promoting sustainable land use practices. The proposed project/programme aims to support these efforts by providing technical assistance and funding for the implementation of these strategies.

Because climatic variations occur at a range of intensities and these variations are all affected by long-term climate changes, the adaptation strategies must be flexible enough to respond to both gradual and abrupt changes or transformational. Incremental adaptation helps to respond to seasonal and interannual variations; systemic adaptation helps to respond to longer term trends in climate change. However, once a system has passed through its transitions when systems are no longer viable (Rosenzweig-Vitousek 2013; Ropp et al., 2016). Farmers have limited ability to adapt to these changes and the impacts of climate change will be felt more acutely by any individual farmer actually faces. As extreme climatic events become more intense and frequent, with the consequences of such events becoming more severe, it is essential that farmers are able to understand how to anticipate climate impacts and cope with physical and monetary losses. Furthermore, as international markets and global food prices continue to increase, countries will need to ensure that their agricultural systems meet these requirements; countries will have to adapt their food supply systems to these challenges to maintain their competitiveness and food security. The Government of Colombia has recognized the importance of climate change adaptation and can therefore experience large economic losses. Most technological packages currently recommended for farmers are not designed with climate change in mind, but rather with a focus on increasing yields.

### Project Overview

The Climate-smart initiative for climate change adaptation and sustainability in prioritized agricultural production systems (rice, corn, banana, sugar cane, potato and cattles in Colombia) has been developed by the Ministry of Agriculture and Livestock (MAG), the National Agricultural Research Institute (INCA), in coordination with producer associations. AGROSAVIA and the Development Bank of Latin America (CAF). The project is being funded through a grant from the Global Environment Facility (GEF) International and CAF, in conjunction with producer associations. ### 8. Beneficiaries and benefits

It is estimated that the Project will benefit directly more than 619,691 people; 194,871 rural producers and their families directly and 347,996 producers indirectly, and it is expected to increase direct employment by 20% (50% of all producers are in the country's provinces, 30% of the country's departments) and 219 municipalities (20% of all municipalities). The total emissions that the project expects to reduce through its ILO is 46.182357 TCO2e.

*The beneficiary producers do not need to be part of one of the gremios to participate in the project. By law, gremios support their members, but also other producers, because the gremios receive public funding to do that.*

B. PROJECT/PROGRAMME INFORMATION

### B.1 Climate Information (mix: 1950 words, approximately 2 pages)

#### 9. The climate problem
The agricultural sector contributed 6.2% of Colombia's total GDP (DANE, 2008), the largest contributor being livestock and poultry production (43%), followed by crops (27%), coffee (11%), forestry and wood extraction (3.5%) and fishing and aquaculture (2.8%). Approximately 43 million hectares are used for agriculture in Colombia, of which 96% is rainfed land and only 4% is irrigated or uses water use (DANE, 2016). There are 2.4 million agricultural production units (UPA), most of which (84%) are located in the departments of Antioquia, Cauca, Caldas, Choco, Huila, Meta and Tolima. The UPA with less than one hectare represents only 0.1% of the total number of UPA while those with more than five hectares represent 95%. The UPA with less than five hectares is 0.2%. The 0.2% of the UPA that are greater than one thousand hectares represent 74% of the departmental area and 83% of the total agricultural production in Colombia (DANE, 2016). Colombia has a diverse range of climatic conditions ranging from paramos in the highlands, through cloud forests to tropical rainforests. The climate is generally characterized by high temperatures and humidity. There shows distinct characteristics in terms of both variable climate and long-term climate change trends; there is no single climate type that can be applied across Colombia. This makes it difficult to predict the impact of the locally. Agricultural production is distributed both in lowlands and mountainous regions where most of the rural population lives. The agricultural sector is highly vulnerable to climate variability and change, with several including flooding, erosion, landslides, droughts, waterlogging above-average temperatures and extreme events such as hurricanes, typhoons, cyclones, and floods (IPCC, 2013; Díaz et al., 2015; Poveda et al., 2017; Barrero-Pérez et al., 2021; Peres et al. 2021). These events are likely to increase with climate change (DEAM, 2016). The agricultural sector is also vulnerable to the impacts of climate variability and change because it is regularly affected by weather-related events, which not only directly reduce production in the field but also damage infrastructure such as roads, bridges, and buildings. This results in a decrease in productivity and yield while leading to scarcity and price increases which hunt the urban poor.

#### 10 Climate projections
The IPCC (2013) has projected that climate change will have an impact of climate change, including higher temperatures, periods of intense rain and drought, and a higher frequency of ever more extreme events. The IPCC's Sixth Assessment Report (AR6) shows that the global average surface temperature is expected to increase by 1.5°C above pre-industrial levels around mid-century under all RCP scenarios except RCP8.5. A clear example of altered climate variability revealed in AR6 is the projected increase in extreme events, such as heatwaves and heavy precipitation (IPCC mode of variation on Earth Call et al., 2019), and while 21st century ENSO projections entail high uncertainty, recent research has shown that ENSO will continue to be a major driver of climate variability at the global scale (Call et al. 2018; Wang et al. 2017; Tran et al., 2021; IPCC, 2021). Relatively early research (e.g., see AR6, Section 3.4) has shown that ENSO will continue to be a major driver of climate variability at the global scale in the ocean-atmospheric dynamics related to ENSO and its impacts. More recent research [post-AR5, feeding into AR6] has also shown that ENSO will continue to be a major driver of climate variability at the global scale (e.g., see Friend et al. 2018). It is expected that future changes to ENSO depend on both mean state change and decadal-scale evolution in the ocean-atmospheric dynamics related to ENSO and its impacts. The AR6 shows that the CMIP6 ensemble, regardless of the lack of signal in the amplification of the Sea Surface Temperature (SST) anomalies associated with ENSO, is consistent with the AR5 finding that ENSO will continue to be a major driver of global warming. For instance, Wang et al. (2017) demonstrate that the frequency of extreme El Niño events has increased in recent decades and that this trend is likely to continue into the future. The AR6 also shows that it find a likely increase in the number of strong equatorial Pacific El Niño events and associated extreme weather events, such as heatwaves and heavy precipitation (IPCC 2021). In addition, the AR6 shows that for all climate models, it is clear that ENSO-related climate variability will continue to challenge agricultural production in all regions of Colombia. The AR6 also shows that the frequency of extreme La Niña events has increased since the mid-20th century and this trend is likely to continue into the future (IPCC 2021). Poveda et al., 2021).

#### 11 Climate variations relevant to agriculture occur at a range of timescales (from days to multi-decades) and it is in these timescales that climate variability and change are most likely to impact agricultural production. # GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL 2 | PAGE 10 OF 75

## Main

- **Low temperature:** Field productivity is reduced in the main maize growing regions of Central America due to low temperatures, with increased disease pressure and decreased irrigation supply. The projected increase in extreme cold nights will further reduce yields.
- **Cattle:** Waterlogging may result from a dry season that is shorter than normal, increasing animal stress.

## Panel discussion

### High temperature
- **Maize:** Field productivity is reduced in the main maize growing regions of Central America due to low temperatures, with increased disease pressure and decreased irrigation supply. The projected increase in extreme cold nights will further reduce yields.
- **Cattle:** Waterlogging may result from a dry season that is shorter than normal, increasing animal stress.

### Dry period
- **Maize:** The effects for the crop are concentrated in the main maize growing regions of Central America due to low temperatures and reduced irrigation supply. Reductions in yield are estimated at between 10% (RCP 2.6) and 35% (RCP 8.5) under RCP 4.5.
- **Cattle:** The effects for the crop are concentrated in the main maize growing regions of Central America due to low temperatures and reduced irrigation supply. Reductions in yield are estimated at between 10% (RCP 2.6) and 35% (RCP 8.5) under RCP 4.5.

## Interactions

- **Maize:** Field productivity is reduced in the main maize growing regions of Central America due to low temperatures, with increased disease pressure and decreased irrigation supply.
- **Cattle:** Waterlogging may result from a dry season that is shorter than normal, increasing animal stress.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 11 OF 75

## Coffee

### Climate change impacts
- **Increased frequency of extreme weather events**: More frequent and intense storms, heatwaves, droughts, and floods.
- **Shift in growing conditions**: Shifts in the optimal temperature range for coffee production.

### Adaptation strategies
- **Improved pest management practices**: Enhanced monitoring and control of pests and diseases.
- **Diversification of crops**: Introducing more resilient varieties or species that can withstand changing climatic conditions.
- **Enhanced irrigation systems**: Implementing efficient water use technologies to cope with reduced rainfall.
- **Soil conservation techniques**: Practices such as mulching, cover cropping, and terracing to improve soil health.

### Benefits
- **Increased productivity (10–25%):** Reduction in yield losses due to climate change.
- **Cost reduction:** Lower input costs associated with improved practices.
- **Improved livelihoods:** Enhanced income from increased coffee production.
- **Enhanced resilience:** Better preparedness for future climate shocks.

## Sugarcane

### Climate change impacts
- **Increased frequency of extreme weather events**: More frequent and intense storms, heatwaves, droughts, and floods.
- **Shift in growing conditions**: Shifts in the optimal temperature range for sugarcane production.

### Adaptation strategies
- **Improved pest management practices**: Enhanced monitoring and control of pests and diseases.
- **Diversification of crops**: Introducing more resilient varieties or species that can withstand changing climatic conditions.
- **Enhanced irrigation systems**: Implementing efficient water use technologies to cope with reduced rainfall.
- **Soil conservation techniques**: Practices such as mulching, cover cropping, and terracing to improve soil health.

### Benefits
- **Increased productivity (10–25%):** Reduction in yield losses due to climate change.
- **Cost reduction:** Lower input costs associated with improved practices.
- **Improved livelihoods:** Enhanced income from increased sugarcane production.
- **Enhanced resilience:** Better preparedness for future climate shocks.

## Maize

### Climate change impacts
- **Increased frequency of extreme weather events**: More frequent and intense storms, heatwaves, droughts, and floods.
- **Shift in growing conditions**: Shifts in the optimal temperature range for maize production.

### Adaptation strategies
- **Improved pest management practices**: Enhanced monitoring and control of pests and diseases.
- **Diversification of crops**: Introducing more resilient varieties or species that can withstand changing climatic conditions.
- **Enhanced irrigation systems**: Implementing efficient water use technologies to cope with reduced rainfall.
- **Soil conservation techniques**: Practices such as mulching, cover cropping, and terracing to improve soil health.

### Benefits
- **Increased productivity (10–25%):** Reduction in yield losses due to climate change.
- **Cost reduction:** Lower input costs associated with improved practices.
- **Improved livelihoods:** Enhanced income from increased maize production.
- **Enhanced resilience:** Better preparedness for future climate shocks.

## Conclusion

Given the extent and scope of climate change impacts, actions are needed that put climate-smart varieties, agricultural technologies, practices, and information services in the hands of farmers so that they are better prepared to adapt. This includes developing more resilient crop varieties, improving soil health through variable-variety and extreme (both which are projected to increase under climate change). Fowler et al., 2021. When is expected in the coming decades. Likewise, greater knowledge production, management and exchange is needed to strengthen institutional capacities, improve institutional capacities, and farmers' knowledge and ability to implement climate-smart technologies.

## B

### 18. The country currently lacks appropriate agri-climate advisory services, and an agricultural research and extension system that can provide producers with time- and site-specific information on how to respond to climate variability and change.

Furthermore, there is only limited institutional capacity to develop technology specifically designed for current and future agricultural conditions in the context of climate change. The country has a strong interest in developing agricultural technology but also to ensure that farmers have access to and confidence in novel, robust farming technologies. This will require an integrated approach involving research institutions, extension services, and farmer systems and continues to follow the outmoded pattern of research followed by validation and then extension.

### 19. Climate change adaptation is a priority for the country.

#### Adaptation needs:

The most relevant impacts of climate variability and change in the agricultural sector in Colombia are: increased frequency and intensity of extreme weather events; reduced water availability; increased occurrence of unexpected risks; reduced number of growing cycles; decreased food and nutritional security; increased vulnerability to pests, diseases, and weeds; and increased costs for inputs and services. These impacts are exacerbated by the lack of adaptation capacity and the limited institutional capacity to provide this service. This increases the vulnerability of the sector considering the changes in precipitation patterns and temperature that will be experienced as a result of climate change.

### 20. Mitigation needs:

According to the TNC, the agricultural sector is the second most important source of greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions in Colombia, contributing 39% of total emissions. Emission sources in the agricultural sector include: livestock production; rice cultivation and processing; oil palm plantations; sugarcane; soybean; coffee; cocoa; and bananas, which represent 55% of irrigation water in agriculture. For its part, the irrigation requirement of temperate crops such as tomatoes, potatoes, and onions is also high.

### 21. With respect to mitigation:

The country has developed a number of measures for the agricultural, forestry and land-use change sectors, considering the following criteria: i) Emission reduction potential; ii) Cost-effectiveness; iii) Technical feasibility; iv) Social acceptability; v) Institutional capacity; vi) Representativeness of agricultural activities in the national territory (area); vii) Reference of NAMA structure (e.g., project, program, or policy); and viii) Alignment with other international commitments (e.g., Paris Agreement 2015-2030). The TNC has identified a number of mitigation measures that can be implemented in Colombia. These include: i) Reducing the emission levels associated with different crop technologies; and ii) Carbon capture technologies have not been selected because they are still at an early stage of development, and there is no certainty about their effectiveness.

### 26. The CISCAP Project:

The CISCAP Project seeks to create institutional, technical and governance capacities that enable the effective adoption of low carbon and climate resilient practices and technologies which integrate climate information in decision-making.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 13 OF 75

B

proceeds to promote in more sustainable production models that are adapted to the country's different economic and social environmental aspects. The CSICAP Project also seeks to address the different barriers that have prevented the adoption of these technologies, such as lack of access to credit, inadequate extension management strategies, different technology transfer approaches and producer association's extension services.

Finally, the CSICAP Project is aligned with nationally-set priorities to strengthen the role of the agricultural sector. It will contribute to the achievement of the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) 2, 13 and 15 by reducing poverty, contributing to a stronger adaptation capacity regarding current and future climate variability and change risks, while also promoting sustainable land management.

The Project is expected to:

- Modernize the agricultural extension system to be climate responsive through the implementation and use of digital tools and other innovative approaches that will increase the efficiency of the extension service as an early warning measure. This will include providing direct technical support to approximately 123,389 producers and indirect technical support to another 405,760 producers in the priority municipalities.
- Develop climate-resilient crop varieties through the use of advanced breeding techniques and resilient and resistant technologies and scale-up to existing technologies (genetic improvement, crop management techniques and other technological options) in 123,111 hectares of land. This will be done by using the resources developed by the CGIAR Agroclimatic Technical Working Groups to reduce crop losses.
- Strengthen the capacity of producers and producer associations models on climate-resilient agriculture considering environmental, social and gender aspects, to ensure adequate technological adoption.

27 Scope: The CSICAP Project is national in scope, and it considers livestock and eight crops: rice, maize, potato, improved varieties and efficient use of fertilizers. It will be implemented in 190 municipalities (including Departmental and Municipality Secretariats) directly benefiting 619,691 people, of which there are more than 194,871 producers and producer associations (253,000 members). This represents a reduction of water use in agricultural models by 1424.320 liters or reducing or avoiding emissions amounting to 15 million CO2e during the project lifespan.

30. **Rolled interventions and shortcomings to be addressed:** MADR, Agrosavia, and the producer associations have a great deal of experience in climate change adaptation through the implementation of agricultural technologies and practices. Colombia's MADR has focused its climate smart agriculture (CSA) efforts on precision farming, including the use of remote sensing for crop monitoring, soil moisture mapping or precision agriculture approaches, and low carbon agricultural practices. These are aligned with sustainable intensification strategies that aim to increase productivity while reducing greenhouse gas emissions and climate resilient interventions at the landscape level (e.g., management of farm and forest boundaries, agroforestry systems, and integrated pest management). The CSA approach also includes incentives for adoption) and food systems (especially consumption patterns and broader climate-informed safety nets. In addition, MADR has been working with producer associations to develop a CSA strategy in the climatic factors. MADR signed a Technical and Scientific Cooperation agreement with the International Center for Tropical Agriculture (CIAT) to support the development of an integrated approach that combines investment in climate-related research, technological development and innovation. This scientific alliance made it possible to identify priority areas for adaptation and mitigation actions, as well as to develop strategies related to climate variability and climate change and improve resource efficiency in some regions productive systems. One of the main objectives is to strengthen the capacity of producer associations and other stakeholders such as research centers, NGOs and producer associations in the productive chain.

under different environmental conditions. In the best of cases, genetic technologies are developed that are applicable in certain contexts. 33 Progress and basic investment. On the other hand, the CSBCP project complements several agricultural and climate change adaptation projects in Nicaragua. The main ones are:

- The Sustainable Livestock Network (NAMA) with the participation of the Colombian Livestock Federation (FEDERIAN), the Center for Research on Sustainable Agricultural Production Systems (CIRPA), CIAT (a World Bank funded project), and the National Institute of Animal Health (INIA). This project is private sector, USD 1.6 million are national public resources and USD 0.8 million international funds.

- The CGIAR Research Program on Climate Change, Agriculture and Food Security (CCAFS) and the International Center for Tropical Agriculture (CIAT) have been working with the National Agricultural Research Institute of Nicaragua in the agricultural sector since CIAT-CIAT led the creation of six Agroclimatic Technical Working Groups (MTA) in 2013. The MTA are composed by representatives from government institutions, private sector and farmers' associations such as the National Federation of Central Legume and Soy Growers (FENAC). The national agricultural research system is represented by INIA, the National Agricultural Research Institute of Nicaragua, Cenicaña, and other participating institutions such as Agropecuaria and universities; it has institutionalized CIAT-CIAT. CGIAR-CCAFS scientists have been working with MTA since 2013 in the development of climate change adaptation strategies for the main crops grown by smallholder farmers (coffee, rice, beans, banana, pineapple, and other fruit-producing regions). CIGAR-CCAFS scientists assessed the information needs in Santander, Córdoba, Tolima, Valle del Cauca, and Antioquia. The results of these assessments were used to develop a set of climate change adaptation strategies for coffee, rice, beans, banana, pineapple, and other fruit-producing regions. CGIAR-CCAFS scientists provided training workshops to MTA's. For example, joint work with FENAC has enabled determining suitable areas for rice production in the municipalities of San Juan del Norte, El Rodeo, and La Unión in the department of Granada; in Santanderia and Costa Norte. These studies determined that the geographic valleys of the Caica and Paita rivers are not suitable for rice production because they have a high risk of flooding during the rainy season. The suitability will be slightly reduced. These analyses have been used to determine climate-change adaptation strategies.

- Other GCF projects may complement geographically the project activities, such as the Amazon Bioeconomy Fund (ABF) and the Scaling up Climate Resilient Water Management Practices for Vulnerable Communities in La Guajira (SCRWMP). The ABF is a multi-donor trust fund that aims to support climate change adaptation and mitigation projects. It will complement each other as the proposed project will work on mitigation (measurement of GHG in rice), while the ABF will focus on adaptation (climate change adaptation strategies for rice production). Both projects work on agriculture information and can interact to share and strengthen knowledge between them.

B.2 Theory of change (max 1000 words, approximately 2 pages plus diagram)


---
**Page 18**
---

34. Aware of the economic, social, and environmental challenges associated with the impacts of climate change in the Colombian agricultural sector, the Government of Colombia, producer associations and other key actors within the agricultural sector have developed a long-term strategy for addressing these challenges through sustainable development pathways. Substantial efforts are needed to ensure systemic and structural change, and this transformation will require the engagement of all stakeholders at different levels, including policy makers and governance challenges that are also relevant to the sustainable and inclusive development of the agricultural sector in Colombia. The following sections describe the components of the CISCAP project and its impacts to achieve a paradigm shift towards a low-carbon, climate-resilient development trajectory. The Theory of Change (ToC) is presented at the end of this document.

35. Project Goal. The overall long-term goal of the CISCAP project is to reduce the vulnerability of agricultural production systems in Colombia to climate change and extreme weather events while reducing GHG emissions from agriculture, and to increase the resilience of these systems to climate change and extreme weather events. At the same time, a national goal is to reduce GHG emissions from agricultural production systems by 20% compared with baseline levels by 2030.

36. Objectives. The project will achieve its overall long-term goal through four interrelated objectives: i) increasing the resilience of agricultural production systems in Colombia to climate change and extreme weather events; ii) reducing GHG emissions from agriculture while improving the sustainability of land use, water management, and biodiversity conservation; iii) increasing the productivity and profitability of smallholder farmers by adapting their farming practices to particular farm conditions, including changes in climate variability and climate change. If novel technological solutions are developed that can be adopted by farmers, then farmers would adopt these technological solutions and become less vulnerable, while using less resources. If proven technologies already exist but have not been widely adopted by farmers, then farmers will adapt their farming practices to particular farm conditions, including changes in climate variability and climate change. If novel technological solutions are developed that can be adopted by farmers, then farmers would adopt these technological solutions and become less vulnerable, while using less resources. If proven technologies already exist but have not been widely adopted by farmers, then farmers will adapt their farming practices to particular farm conditions, including changes in climate variability and climate change.

37. | Phase | Description |

#### Gaps and barriers

Based on the expected project outcomes, results, the project, through its activities, addresses gaps and barriers in the Colombian agricultural sector that are preventing the adoption of climate change mitigation practices and technologies.

sector for promoting sustainable use of resources (water, soil, agricultural inputs, etc.) while developing and implementing management practices of cultivation and agricultural technologies that increase resilience to climate change and variability. The barriers are related to the lack of information and knowledge about climate change and climate variable and change. There is limited basic information or agronomic capacity, and the deficient technical know-how of local farmers and extension services. In addition, there is a lack of understanding of how to transfer this knowledge to local farmers themselves, where there are difficulties in making climate-smart decisions with precision, scale-and on time. There is also a lack of infrastructure for the collection and dissemination of information, as well as the existence of gaps in the crop cycles, analysis and dissemination of information. At the same time, there is reduced coverage of agricultural extension services and limited access to credit facilities. In addition, there are limitations in capacity in digital agriculture and data analysis with machine learning (M) and artificial intelligence (AI). There is also a lack of infrastructure for the collection and dissemination of information, as well as the existence of gaps in the use of tools to data.

### 4.3 Institutional arrangements and governance model that provides blanket recommendations rather than site- and time-specific guidelines. Gremios are a striking feature of the major crops in Colombian agriculture were all farmers belong to one or more gremios. These organizations have historically been dominated by men, and remain with highly centralized structures that pay relatively little attention to variation over space and time of the environment. The lack of flexibility in these institutions is reflected in their limited capacity to provide extension services and often have a minimal say in the overall strategies and management of the powerful growers' associations. As such, they are not well positioned to support the development of climate-smart agriculture on a climate resilient sound agriculture are not available. As a result, there is limited technical and institutional capacity for the adoption of climate-smart agricultural practices in Colombia. Furthermore, information and knowledge are not shared, and power of growers is not effectively utilized.

### 4.3.1 Institutional arrangements and governance model that provides blanket recommendations rather than site- and time-specific guidelines

#### a) Inclusion of women farmers in decision-making processes
Inclusion of women farmers in decision-making processes is critical for the adoption of some of these technologies may have a reducing climate vulnerability and GHG emissions.

#### b) Housework and unpaid care work activities that are often on rural women, which consistently limits their access to productive resources. In addition, women farmers face additional barriers such as lack of land tenure security, women farmers and ranchers have less access to production factors, such as land, credit and technology, which reduces the potential for adoption of climate-smart agricultural practices.

## B

### 3.3 Capacity building needs to facilitate the strengthening of agriculture business models by enabling ownership transfer and use of the knowledge generated in components I and 2 through innovative, inclusive and digitally enabled approaches. The following risks will be addressed:

- Risk (v) which relates to the lack of awareness among farmers and gremios and farmers efforts to de-risk agricultural production systems and incentivizing them to use project’s knowledge and technology will be addressed by Result 3.1 through which business models in prioritized value chains will be analyzed to identify opportunities for risk reduction, including financial instruments.
- Risk (vi) which relates to the lack of awareness among farmers and gremios on how to access financing institutions; barrier (vii) will be addressed by Result 3.2, which will allow gremios to improve their knowledge on accessing financing institutions.
- Risks (ix), (x), (xi) and (xii) which relate to the lack of awareness among farmers and gremios regarding the use of digital tools and services; barrier (xiii) and (xiv) will be addressed by Component 1, 2 and 3 as they implement related activities to generate awareness.

### 4.0 Risks

Among the identified risks for this project is that farmers and ranchers tend to be very conservative when adopting new technologies and practices. This may result in a slow rate of adoption of the project’s innovations, introduction of new profitable procedures or systems. There are also possible incorrect design or management of field-based activities, which could lead to failure of the project’s innovations. The project will carry out training on crop technologies. Potential issues regarding local governance or mechanisms that may limit sustainability of the interventions and their impact on the environment and biodiversity include lack of adequate infrastructure for land and tools. Finally, the project will carry out activities in remote areas with difficult access, which represents a risk for the project’s innovations.

### 5.0 Assumptions

It is noteworthy that the project outcomes will be achieved through implementation of the project activities as planned. The assumptions are that: (a) Farmers and gremios will be adequately trained on new technological packages and best management practices; (b) Key stakeholders such as farmer associations and producers’ organizations, extension services, research institutions and other relevant actors will be involved in the project’s activities; (c) Regular meetings and information exchanges between farmers and extension agents are maintained and renewed periodically; (d) Technicians participate in the training activities and are adequately trained on new technologies and practices; (e) The communication channels for information exchange are effective/efficient to enable and speed up the adoption of low-carbon intensive agriculture practices; (f) Farmers and gremios will be able to access the project’s innovations through the dissemination mechanisms that have been put in place; (g) There is interest in activity participating in the project. Farmers attend the trainings and know how to use the information, VI technologies and services provided by the project; (h) The project’s innovations are adequately disseminated and the organizations / institutions are able to access it; and field activities for variety development are well organized.

### B.3 Project/programme description (max. 20 words, approximately 4 pages)

#### 56. Project components

The project comprises three interconnected components and their respective activities: (1) Digital agriculture and climate services for rural modernization with an emphasis on adaptation to climate change; (2) Agricultural extension services for rural modernization with an emphasis on adaptation to climate change; (3) Innovative and inclusive business models for rural modernization.

#### Component 1. Digital agriculture and climate services for rural modernization with an emphasis on adaptation to climate change

The project seeks to modernize the agricultural production systems in Colombia by introducing digital climate information services for technicians and producers. The objective is to improve decision-making at the producer level, through the use of digital climate information services that will provide real-time data on weather conditions, soil moisture levels, crop growth stages, pest and disease forecasts, fertilizer requirements, etc. Digital agriculture and climate services for rural modernization with an emphasis on adaptation to climate change will be implemented in three targeted value chains: coffee, banana and sugarcane.

#### Component 2. Agricultural extension services for rural modernization with an emphasis on adaptation to climate change

The project seeks to modernize the agricultural production systems in Colombia by introducing digital climate information services for technicians and producers. The objective is to improve decision-making at the producer level, through the use of digital climate information services that will provide real-time data on weather conditions, soil moisture levels, crop growth stages, pest and disease forecasts, fertilizer requirements, etc. Digital agriculture and climate services for rural modernization with an emphasis on adaptation to climate change will be implemented in three targeted value chains: coffee, banana and sugarcane.

#### Component 3. Innovative and inclusive business models for rural modernization

The project seeks to modernize the agricultural production systems in Colombia by introducing digital climate information services for technicians and producers. The objective is to improve decision-making at the producer level, through the use of digital climate information services that will provide real-time data on weather conditions, soil moisture levels, crop growth stages, pest and disease forecasts, fertilizer requirements, etc. Digital agriculture and climate services for rural modernization with an emphasis on adaptation to climate change will be implemented in three targeted value chains: coffee, banana and sugarcane.

**gender information**, and the empowerment of producer associations to deliver recommendations on adaptation and mitigation measures to producers. It is considered that rice, coffee and sugarcane crops are the most advanced in implementing the SIRA platform for information collection and processing systems. The SIRA system has been implemented in the case of panels sugarcane; the SIRA platform for information collection is currently available. Therefore, the project will focus on strengthening the extension services to producers and improving the information processing and analysis system and developing the system to produce outputs. In the case of rice and coffee, the project will develop a database and an information processing and analysis system using existing information collection instruments related to some variables, including water availability for cultivation**.

58 As far as the SIRA platform is concerned, the project will focus on strengthening the extension services in an effort to promote the adoption and use of these instruments as inputs for the National Unified Agriculture and Rural Rural Information System (SINAR) and the National Agricultural Extension Service (SENA). The specific aspect will be clearly defined in the different implementation agreements with the strategic partners research institutions, producer associations, and the SIRA platform. The project will also work closely with the CGIAR Alliance. This team will coordinate communications with the producer associations teams and AGROVISA. It includes:

- **Activity 1.1.3 - Organize the information from the different repositories and producer association databases (public and private) into a single database that can be accessed by producers, extension services, and other access platforms through remote sensing or other sources. This includes: i) developing a data collection strategy for the SIRA platform; ii) developing an information processing system to collect and disseminate knowledge; iii) developing a protocol to integrate databases with existing crop information; iv) processing and classifying the information collected from different sources (SIRA, SINAR, SEÑA, CGIAR, producer associations, etc.); v) analyzing data using machine learning and computational optimization methods; vi) disseminating results through extension services.

- **Activity 1.2 - Develop a bot-based digital instrument to strengthen the extension service. There are plans to develop a bot that will be able to answer frequently asked questions in conversations between farmers and technicians. This will facilitate sharing the information collected from different sources (SIRA, SINAR, SEÑA, CGIAR, producer associations, etc.) and pattern recognition models in tasks that producers and technicians have exchanged; i) engaging with potential users of the bot to identify their needs and expectations for the bot; ii) developing a bot that can be used more frequently requiring decision making, as well as the most frequently used information channels. This will be developed through a participatory process involving farmers, technicians, and other stakeholders using automatic wizard to exchange agronomic information with producers, through chats or text messages on platforms such as WhatsApp, Telegram, etc.

- **Activity 1.1.3 - Design and develop the entire data mining and analysis operating system that will allow access to the information collected from different sources (SIRA, SINAR, SEÑA, CGIAR, producer associations) for analysis and modeling processes, in an effort to encourage more robust recommendations and more agile and effective decision-making by producers. This includes: i) developing a mobile application (smartphones and drones) and non-remote sensors (leaf moisture sensors, electroconductivity, leaf moisture sensors); ii) evaluating the use of remote sensing for crop monitoring; iii) developing an information system that allows searching for information, downloading, and processing it; iv) analyzing the information and validating the results.

- **Activity 1.1.4 - Train technicians in the use and application of advanced data analysis methods, including: i) building a database with the most relevant variables to be collected from different sources (SIRA, SINAR, etc.) at AGROVISA and other centers of excellence; ii) developing skills in the use of data mining, evolutionary computing, pattern recognition models, and machine learning algorithms for crop monitoring; iii) validating and implementing a module based on graphical interfaces to facilitate data analysis and consultation with producers. This will be done through training sessions that include practical exercises with producers with an ethnic and gender focus and develop and implement dissemination means, including workshops, seminars, etc.

60 **Result 12 - Agroclimatic services in place and/or strengthened**. This will support the increased adoption and implementation of SIRA platforms for information collection and processing systems by producer associations. As a result, the project will aim to provide timely and reliable information to more than one million users (considered as producers) through SINAR, SEÑA, CGIAR, and other access platforms.

*In the case of coffee cultivation, the currently available SIRA system handles a good volume of variables and therefore, it was not included in this component. Nonetheless, information from the SIRA system will be analyzed in other components of this project.

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 22 OF 75

B

Project on providing technical support in the implementation of adaptation and mitigation measures to about 43,000 of these producers, who are considered direct project beneficiaries. The project will focus up-on these beneficiaries and will be implemented through a series of activities that aim at strengthening the capacity of the early warning platforms that provide accurate and timely information for farmers by generating climate predictions, using simulation models and other tools.

### Activity 1.2.1: Design and/or strengthen agronomic information services

A first step in this activity is to expand the coverage of services to other crops (potato, panels sugarcane, livestock, banana, and pineapple) and to improve the quality of climate predictions for these crops by using new generation climate and related variables. Data collection, storage and analysis, climate predictions, crop modeling, climate risk management, irrigation planning and optimization, and dissemination of information services will be strengthened with new-generation models and improved performance of climate predictions and crop modeling. Likewise, the project will support the development of a protocol for the use of climate information in decision-making processes related to water and factor use planning in irrigation systems. In this case, it is necessary to move forth in developing calibration and validation protocols for the use of climate information services.

### Activity 1.2.2: Develop more accurate and precise climate and weather prediction models

This result will be under the leadership of the Climate Action team of the CIAT-Deversity Alliance, which will coordinate with other partners to ensure that the activities are carried out in a coordinated manner.

#### Sub-activities:

* Activity 1.2.1: Design and/or strengthen agronomic information services.
    * To expand the coverage of information services to reduce the agronomic risk for new productive systems (potatoes, panels sugarcane, livestock, banana, and pineapple) by using new-generation climate models and improving the performance of climate predictions and crop modeling.
* Activity 1.2.2: Develop more accurate and precise climate and weather prediction models.

### Activity 1.3: Implement early warning platforms for climate services

This activity will be implemented in two phases:

#### Phase I (2024-2025):

* Integrating agronomic forecasts in the design and implementation of early warning platforms that use climate information to provide recommendations on decision-making processes related to water, factor use planning, and irrigation.
    * Useability tests with producers of agronomic platforms and mass dissemination systems.

#### Phase II (2026-2031):

* Implementing an integrated early warning platform for climate services in the region of the AcumateColecta (www.acumatecolecta.org), SPA (sugarcane), and ASISTEGAN (livestock) platforms.
    * Useability tests with producers of agronomic platforms and mass dissemination systems.

### Activity 1.4: Strengthen the capacity for climate services in the region

This activity will be implemented through a series of activities that aim at strengthening the capacity of early warning platforms to provide accurate and timely information on meteorological information, analysis of climate change signals, nutrient balance models. In the case of sugarcane, it is necessary to develop a protocol for the use of climate information services in decision-making processes to mitigate the effects of climate variability on production.

* Monitoring and evaluation will be carried out as part of the impact evaluation component and will focus on a representative sample of these platforms.
    * See Appendix A in Annex 2.

# Component 2: Genetic improvement, crop management techniques and other technological options and their potential for adaptation to climate change

## Activity 2.1.1: Development of state-of-the-art phenotyping platform designed to identify low carbon production and climate-resilient varieties of rice, maize, potatoes, melons, sorghum, beans, soybeans, wheat, barley, cassava, and bananas

### Activities
- i) Identification of the most promising genotypes for adaptation and mitigation for rice, maize, livestock (varieties that are tolerant or resistant to water deficit and drought that demand less water and have greater efficiency in nitrogen use). This includes: (a) selecting and evaluating advanced varieties under climate change conditions; (b) characterizing and creating the elite parent lines; (c) new crosses of elite parents with other genotypes; (d) selection of lines with better performance.
- ii) Evaluation of the most promising genotypes for adaptation and mitigation for rice, maize, livestock (varieties that are tolerant or resistant to water deficit and drought that demand less water and have greater efficiency in nitrogen use). ### 68. Result 3: Improving efficient use of water resources, restoring soil properties, and improving GHG mitigation practices by farmers and gardens through the adoption of new climate smart practices and technologies.

This component will contribute to increasing the resilience and productivity of smallholder farms in terms of food security, livelihoods, resilience and mitigation in the targeted value chains for different types of farmers. This considers the validation and incorporation of climate-smart agricultural (CSA) practices and technologies that improve soil fertility properties, reducing GHG emissions or increasing carbon capture, while enabling productivity increases. As a result of these interventions, it is expected that there will be an increase in food security and livelihoods as well as reduction of GHG emissions. It is expected that there will be a reduction of 324,297 tons CO₂e during the project period.

### Under this component, sustainable management practices will be formulated and implemented at a large scale.

#### Activity 2.21. Quantifying the baseline water footprint in six (6) crops (potatoes, sugarcane, pasture sugarcane, banana, plantain and coffee), optimization of irrigation systems (rice, potato, plantain and banana), optimization of processing systems (coffee and potatoes), and livestock production.

#### Activity 2.22. Quantifying the baseline carbon footprint for seven (7) crops (rice, potato, sugarcane, pasture sugarcane, banana, plantain and coffee).

This activity includes: i) Collecting information on activities in cultivation and transformation processes; ii) Building a database of water use practices and crop management practices at the level of irrigation districts.

#### Activity 2.23. Developing models of farmers to identify water harvesting points in rice, potato cane, pasture sugarcane, bananas, and coffee production system. This includes: i) Collecting spatial and hydrological information in basic data layers; ii) Building a database of water use practices and crop management practices at the level of irrigation districts.

#### Activity 2.24. Developing models of farmers to identify water harvesting points in rice, potato cane, pasture sugarcane, bananas, and coffee production system. This includes: i) Collecting spatial and hydrological information in basic data layers; ii) Building a database of water use practices and crop management practices at the level of irrigation districts.

#### Activity 2.25. Evaluation of efficient water-use practices in conventional and improved water management and transformation processes (e.g., milling of cane panela); potato cane, pasture sugarcane, banana, coffee crops and livestock production systems.

This activity includes: i) Identifying productive technologies considering gender and socioeconomic restated variables; ii) Implementing climate-smart agricultural (CSA) practices and technologies that improve soil fertility properties, reducing GHG emissions or increasing carbon capture, while enabling productivity increases. As a result of these interventions, it is expected that there will be an increase in food security and livelihoods as well as reduction of GHG emissions.

#### Activity 2.26. Evaluation of GHG emission mitigation practices in agronomic management and transformation processes (e.g., milling of cane panela); potato cane, pasture sugarcane, banana, coffee crops and livestock production systems.

This activity includes: i) Identifying productive technologies considering gender and socioeconomic restated variables; ii) Implementing climate-smart agricultural (CSA) practices and technologies that improve soil fertility properties, reducing GHG emissions or increasing carbon capture, while enabling productivity increases. As a result of these interventions, it is expected that there will be an increase in food security and livelihoods as well as reduction of GHG emissions.

#### Activity 2.27. Landscape restoration in sugarcane cultivation and livestock systems. The activities that will be developed include: i) Collecting information on the current state of land use; ii) Identifying areas with potential for improvement, such as the implementation of biocultural corridors, riverine corridors, living fences, intensive and non-intensive silvo-pastoral systems, pasture management practices, etc.; iii) Developing a database of water use practices and crop management practices at the level of irrigation districts.

#### Activity 2.28. Landscape restoration in sugarcane cultivation and livestock systems. The activities that will be developed include: i) Prioritizing activities and identifying potential intervention areas and landscape units through indicator parameter analysis; ii) Designing indicators for monitoring and evaluation, including gender and age engagement of women farmers; iii) Coverage mapping; iv) Implementation of the identified interventions.

### Activity 3.1.07: Massive implementation of agronomic management technologies and transformation processes that enable more efficient use of water and promote low-carbon development in rice, maize, potato, sugarcane, and coffee production systems. This component will focus on the following activities:

- i) Identifying and selecting producers for implementation.
- ii) Training producers and other actors in the implementation and maintenance of these technologies.
- iii) Ensuring that all relevant stakeholders are involved in the design and implementation of climate-smart agriculture focused on the tasks they perform within each cropchain (e.g. seed selection, diversification of crops, water management).
- iv) Generating inclusive participatory processes with producers.
- v) Generating dissemination material suitable for different types of audiences.

### Component 3: Innovative and inclusive business models through modernized innovation systems and a modernized financial system

This component aims to support the development of science-based business models and the modernization of innovation systems, which will be achieved by:

1. Strengthening the capacity of public institutions and producer-producers through digitally-enabled tools and approaches for sharing knowledge and experiences.
2. Ensuring that all relevant stakeholders are involved in the design and implementation of climate-smart agriculture focused on the tasks they perform within each cropchain (e.g. seed selection, diversification of crops, water management).
3. Generating inclusive participatory processes with producers.

This component aims to support the development of science-based business models and the modernization of innovation systems through two-way digitally enabled tools in this component, the project will closely engage with financial institutions both national and international, as well as other relevant stakeholders for de-risking agricultural investments within the framework of the project, highlighting the need for tailored financial mechanisms to support climate-smart agriculture.

This component will contemplate the relationship with the Departmental Agricultural Extension Program (DPEA) providing technical assistance in the development of business models and the generation of new knowledge and technology transfer processes. This component will result in two results areas:

### Result 3.1: Increased business models that provide farmers with accessible profits and improved GHG mitigation

Component 3.1.01: Develop a gender-sensitive value chain analysis to identify novel and inclusive business models.

- Activity 3.1.1. Strengthen producers’ capacities to use digitally enabled tools that improve the provision of services and access to information, thereby increasing their ability to respond to farmers' demands while strengthening processes of allocation of resources to services for farmers.
- Assessment of agroecological conditions and climate resilience.
- Development of a business model for the decision-making process to cost-effectively provide services to farmers in a way that is financially viable and provides an advantage.

For example, the projects under AGROBIO have clear and unified standards for the selection process and technology transfer. #### B

Selection of beneficiary producers. ## Framework for engagement and interaction between MADR, as ECA, and the main procured party

The Project Coordinating Team (PCT) will have main theme coordinators and cross-cutting theme leaders. The project will have a group of six theme leaders, comprised of CPT staff based in the city of Palma, Valle del Caucá. These theme leaders will be responsible for developing the work plans and budgets for implementing the activities of each of the project components. Each will have a budget subject to the project financial framework. The theme leaders will also be responsible for monitoring and evaluating the implementation of the activities of each component and ensuring the achievement of their respective outputs and goals.

- **Main theme leader 1:** Water Efficiency Use and Low Emission Technologies Coordinator
- **Main theme leader 2:** Water Management and Sanitation Coordinator
- **Cross-cutting theme leader 1:** Knowledge Management and Capacity Building Coordinator.
- **Cross-cutting theme leader 2:** Environmental, Social and Gender Affairs Coordinator.

The direct contracting of CIAT will be carried out based on the Colombian national regulations as established in literal a) number 4 of article 30 of Law No. 597 of 1991; literal b) number 6 of article 32 of Law No. 802 of 2005, articles 2 and 17 of Decree - Law 591 of 1991, article 33 of Law 1298 of 2000.

## B

### Strategic partners for project implementation will have fifteen (15) strategic partners, of which three are research institutions and twelve are producer organizations.

The project will be divided into eight (8) crop coordinators, one for each crop. These coordinators are renowned experts in their respective crops who will be responsible for coordinating the activities to be held at the local level with international journals. These professionals will be responsible for integrating all of the topics within each crop in a single document that will be submitted to the project team and the GCF. The coordinators will also be responsible for giving direction and making proposals for improvement, adjustments or coordination activities as appropriate, given that they will not have a budget allocated for costs other than the cost of their time dedicated to the project.

The project will be implemented in two (2) main production areas: Cercica (coffee), Cercicata (sugarcane), CIAT and CMMYT staff will be based in the city of Cali. The sugarcane research station is located in the municipality of San Antonio del Tequendama (Cali), and Cercicata in the municipality of Candelaria, Valle del Caucá. Figure B.4.2 shows the structure of CIAT's work team.

### Strategic partners

The project will have fifteen (15) strategic partners for project implementation, of which three are research institutions and twelve are producer organizations. For the execution of the CSCCAP Project: CIAT will work in coordination with the following 10 generics: Fedecafé (rice), Fenafele (maize), Fedegan (livestock), Fedepecueros (poultry), Federación de Productores de Café (coffee), Federación de Productores de Cercica (sugarcane), Auguras, Abasoma, and Adhoofucol (muscaceos). Additionally, there are three (3) research institutions that will be part of the project: the International Center for Tropical Agriculture (CIAT), the Colombian Institute for Agroscience and Technology (Instituto Colombiano de Agrociencia y Tecnología - ICAT), and the International Maize and Wheat Improvement Center (CIMMYT) and the Center for Research on Sustainable Agricultural Systems (Sustainable Agriculture Research and Education - SARE). These institutions will have a role in the implementation of project activities and therefore will be assigned a budget that will be subject to outside evaluation. The remaining twelve strategic partners are producer organizations, which will be responsible for the continuity and sustainability of the project actions once the project is completed.

### Figure B-4.2 Organigram of the main procured party (CIAT)

The project will have eight (8) crop coordinators, one for each crop, with a high level of recognition and outreach to producers and a high level of technical knowledge of the production activities in their respective crops. The coordinators will be responsible for giving direction and making proposals for improvement, adjustments or coordination activities as appropriate, given that they will not have a budget allocated for costs other than the cost of their time dedicated to the project implementation through the use of public funds assigned to them ("fondos parafiscales").

## Framework for working with strategic partners

### Project supervision structure
Two bodies will be formed to supervise the project technical and financial execution: a Monitoring Committee and a Steering Committee. The following is the governance structure, its conformation and functions of the different instances.

#### Follow-up Committee
- Steering Committee
- Project Executing Unit
- Project Coordinating Team

### Project governance structure

#### Monitoring Committee
The Monitoring Committee will be in charge of approving the progress on technical execution, reviewing project reports, adopting conclusions, making recommendations for observations anterior requesting adjustments. The Monitoring Committee has annual meetings. The Committee will be made up of representatives of MADS (Director of Climate Change and Sustainable Development), MINVINCENDA (one delegate), INVIVI (one delegate) and the Alliance of Universities International and CIAT (one delegate). Invited actors include MADS (Director of Climate Change and Sustainable Development).

#### Steering Committee
Its functions are to review the annual operating plan, provide strategic guidance to the project, approve the project budget, adopt policies, identify and recommend strategic partnerships to assist in scaling up project actions, review progress reports, make recommendations for adjustments, and adopt conclusions. The Steering Committee will meet every six months. The Steering Committee will be made up of representatives of MADS (Director of Climate Change and Sustainable Development), MINVINCENDA (one delegate) and the Alliance of Universities International and CIAT (one delegate). Invited actors include INVIVI (the project coordinator).

## 50. GCF for Fund Mobilization

Given the high uncertainty and vulnerability to unexpected weather events, agricultural investments are key. The GCF investment will help increase the adaptive capacity of farmers and ranchers and improve their resilience in the face of climate change. This is particularly important given that Colombia has a large agricultural sector, while supporting GHG emissions reductions. Colombia has suffered the consequences of climate events that have affected its crops and livestock production. In addition to this, the country has been unable to implement policies to improve a large part of the agricultural infrastructure, productivity, and competitiveness. It is necessary to improve the design of public goods in agriculture, which are essential for the development of the sector at national level and for farmers and ranchers to avoid and prepare for climate events. However, the nature of the investments required to achieve these goals is not well understood by the Colombian Government or by the private sector.

On the other hand, the capital markets in Colombia are not ready yet to assume the risks of new technologies or development projects that require a long-term horizon. The GCF can help mobilize resources for this purpose and, furthermore, in making the technologies developed by the project more widespread as they are adopted on a larger scale.

Although the pilot experiences show very high returns on investments in CICAD programs (see Annex 23 for the details of the financial analysis), there is still a lack of experience with these types of public goods at scale and, become the norm. Although the farmers group sees the advantages in these investments, they do not have enough resources to implement them. The same applies to the private sector, which has limited access to credit facilities, so they need support from the public sector to make these public goods investments. Most of the reasons are similar: lack of financial instruments and financing capacity, both of the public sector and from the private sector (e.g., agricultural banks). In addition, there is a lack of knowledge about the potential benefits of the combination of technologies and practices, both areas where the participation of the GCF can help to change the current paradigm. The project will contribute to the development of these public goods in Colombia by promoting the adoption of low-carbon technologies and practices.

In this context, the Government has included a domestic recovery strategy of the Government of Colombia post-COVID 19, investments are being promoted to be resilient and low in carbon, a bet that the Government is making after the pandemic.

### 50.1 Justification of financial instruments

The proposed financial instruments (loans and grants) were selected for their ability to provide the required resources at scale, become the norm. Although the farmers group sees the advantages in these investments, they do not have enough resources to implement them. The same applies to the private sector, which has limited access to credit facilities, so they need support from the public sector to make these public goods investments.

### 50.2 Justification of the level of concessionality

GCF offers a unique opportunity for lending (low interest loans and grants) and grant financing at concessional terms. The project is structured in such a way that there is a minimum of concessionality to make the investments in the project viable, but still allows the GCF to achieve its objectives.

### 50.3 Justification of the level of concessionality

The project has been designed with the following criteria: (i) the reduction of GHG emissions; (ii) the increase of Economic and Financial Analysis (Annex 3); the reduction in the concessionality would make the project not viable. When considering the cost-benefit analysis, it is clear that the project will be financially viable if the following conditions are met: (a) reduced levels of grants (75% reduction of the project is not viable for the majority of the grantee institutions); (b) increased levels of loans; (c) reduced levels of co-financing; (d) improved land use, soil improvement, big data, GHG emission measurement, etc.) considered by the project. That is, reimbursement would be required to make the project financially viable.

### 50.4 Justification of the level of concessionality

The project is structured in such a way that there is a minimum of concessionality to make the investments in the project viable, but still allows the GCF to achieve its objectives.

#### B

90. CAF has a history of supporting the Government of Colombia and the agricultural sector for the implementation of innovative ideas. In this case, the proposed project has been designed in close consultation with the MADR at an early stage (Annex 6) and during the preparation phase (Annexes 7 and 8). The results of these consultations and discussions (detailed in Annex 7), combined with the experience of the pilot project and the technologies tested in them, have led to a proposal that is based on a solid approach and a set of interventions that they are implemented with strong participation from farmers' groups. This ensures that the investments made will be sustainable over time.

91. A key innovative feature of the project is the strengthening of the capacities of the gremios, through inclusive and participatory processes (Components 1 and 2, Results 3.1), and the strengthening of agricultural extension systems (Results 1.1 and 2.1) to ensure that the investments made are sustainable over time. This will be achieved by the design and replication of the adoption and mitigation measures introduced for each production chain. In the case of public investment in infrastructure, this will be done through the development of a model for the design, construction, extension systems, as well as all of the farmer associations within the project. By internalizing these investments, Agropecuarias de Colombia (AGROCOL) and the gremios will have the capacity to replicate them on their own.

92. The longer-term sustainability of the project's investments will be guaranteed through a combination of elements that include: the strengthening of capacities in the gremios; the design and replication of adoption and mitigation measures for each production chain; the involvement of the national government through the MADR and Agropecuarias de Colombia (AGROCOL) and the farmer groups to manage and obtain social, economic, and environmental benefits from the investments made by the project; the strengthening of the capacities of the gremios in the design and replication of adoption and mitigation measures for each production chain; the involvement of the national government through the MADR and Agropecuarias de Colombia (AGROCOL) and the farmer groups to manage and obtain social, economic, and environmental benefits from the investments made by the project.

93. The gremios will be an active participant of the project formulation (as described in the Annex 7 of project consultations). They have participated in the design of the activities, the resources required to implement them, the implementation of the project, and the monitoring and evaluation of its results. The gremios will also be responsible for the implied investment costs; the O&M of each activity during and after project completion. The current commitment of the gremios is to ensure that the investments made are sustainable over time by strengthening their capacities in the control and operation of all of the tools, technologies, and processes developed under the project. In fact, project design and implementation will be carried out with the active participation of the gremios; this will continue until the project completion. This transition will be supported by the instructional strengthening through local consultants covered for the project.

94. At the end of the project, the equipment and infrastructure developed by the project will continue to be in the name of the gremios (Federación de Gremios de Productores de Café y Cacao del Norte de Colombia) (contrato de comités) in which the gremios manages the assets and services (including data platforms) under their responsibility, according to the terms established by the Colombian government and the applicable regulations. This mechanism has been used in several projects between MADR and the gremios; including the project for the development of a coffee value chain in the Norte de Santander region, which was implemented on behalf of the gremios (Federación y Fenalce), which continued and expanded the work carried out with them.

# C2. Financing by component

The project is financed with a mix of financing resources from GCF (Development Bank of Latin America), the Government of Colombia, agricultural research institutions (Alianza Bovinera CIAT, CYMNET and CIFAP), and the private sector (Café Fina). The financing plan of the Project broken down by sources of resources (loan, grant from the GCF and local co-financing) is presented in Annex 4. The total amount of resources required for the project is USD 135 million: one million GCF funds (USD 25 million in senior loan and USD 48.26 million in grant), CIFAP contributes to the Project with a grant of USD 70 million, CYMNET contributes with a grant of USD 19.5 million, and the private sector as the Alliance between Bovinera International and CIAT and the farmer groups is USD 16.3 million.

The summary of the project costs for each of the Components and Products, by type of source (GCF or co-financing) and payment method (loan or grant), is presented in Annex 4 and the execution schedule is presented in Annex 5. In Annex 6, the budget provides the breakdown by type of expenditure (e.g., personnel costs, travel expenses). The project will be implemented through a local implementing entity, which will be responsible for the deployment of local consultants and staff to support the implementation of the project activities. Thus, almost 37 percent of the budget is allocated to personnel costs (including salaries, benefits, and allowances) for the technicians that will work in the field to provide technical capacity building to local farmers to implement the project activities. The remaining 63 percent of the budget is divided into three categories: (1) research (both scientific advisors and scientists), which represents 19 percent of the budget; (2) field visits and the development of tools, which represent 40 percent of the budget; and (3) dissemination of results and outcomes of the project. International consultants represent 2 percent of the budget. Local consultants represent 6 percent of the budget.

# D. EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA

This section assesses the performance of the project against the investment criteria as set out in the GCF's Initial Investment Framework.

## D.1. Impact potential (max. 500 words, approximately one page)

It is estimated that the Project will have positive impacts on more than $178,691 direct beneficiaries (producers and their families) through increased growth of crops and production of rice, cereals and legumes, potatoes, coffee, sugar cane and panela cane, bananas and plantains. The direct beneficiaries include producers, professionals from the farmer association, and professionals from other entities, respectively. The production area associated with these direct beneficiaries and that the project would cover is estimated at 167,907 hectares (ha) of land in total, which corresponds to 25% of the total agricultural area cultivated by farmers and more than 500,000 during the useful life of the technologies. It is important to note that based on the figures from Table D.1.1., the project would cover only 37% of the total beneficiaries (direct beneficiaries) in terms of total producers at the national level, while the total indirect beneficiaries would be equivalent to a 1 percent of total agricultural households (out of a total of 108,000). As previously mentioned, at the geographical level, the project will cover 22 departments (95% of the total departments in Colombia) and 34 municipalities (76.5%), which corresponds to 100% of the total agricultural households for the different beneficiaries (producers, professionals from the farmer association, and professionals from other entities, respectively). The project will cover 82% of the total producers at the national level.
