## D.2 Paradigm shift potential (max. 500 words, approximate 1 page)

The project seeks to change the current trajectory of agricultural practices and technologies, which for some, have limited investment in innovative proposals such as the use of digital media to disseminate climate information, or the development of new varieties that are more resilient to climate change.

In this sense, the project will focus on the adaptation of existing crops and livestock systems by farmers to adapt their practices and technologies towards more investments, resilient, and low carbon. In that sense, the project is transferred from a perspective of adaptation at the farm level to an approach where the entire agricultural sector begins to replicate the project experience.

Partnership: The Ministry of Agriculture (MAIC) and CACT have already implemented a pilot project that includes rice and cotton in some regions of Colombia such as Villavicencio, Yopal, Aguaragüe, Bagatá, Saitala, and San José del Guaviare. This pilot project has been successful in the adaptation of new varieties to climate change and replication to other crop regions and cover more products through the mechanisms of agricultural extension and promotion of public-private partnerships.

The project will be implemented with the participation of 102 municipalities (35% of the total number of farmer associations involved) (Project 1.1), AgroRural, and CGIAR institutions, and the experiences and lessons learned from this pilot project will be scaled up to other regions in Colombia through the support of the country's departments) and 219 municipalities (20% of the Colombian municipalities). It is expected that once the project is implemented, it will cover more than 581,471 hectares. The project will be implemented with the support of MAIC, AgroRural, and CACT, will continue to expand the adoption of the technologies to other crops and livestock systems in Colombia.

The project seeks to generate a paradigm shift by scaling up and replicating those technologies. For example, due to the level of investment that will be made in climate change adaptation, it is expected that the extension program will be able to disseminate these technologies and best practices.

Potential for knowledge sharing and learning: The project will generate the necessary know-how to build new agricultural technological capabilities, empower producers to launch similar projects on their own. This will be done through a public-private partnership model in which the Ministry of Agriculture (MAIC) will lead the key public and private actors to strengthen the capacities that allow influencing future improvements in public policy.

The project will also generate knowledge by disseminating experiences from the pilot project, as well as by keeping farmers associations to keep them updated on the progress of the project, presence in social networks and the development of a website. The project will also be able to disseminate its results through the dissemination of its solid track record and research capacity, worldwide presence and strategic alliance with the productive sector.

Specifically, the project will generate knowledge by disseminating experiences from the pilot project, as well as by disseminating successful experiences and show how to participate and take better advantage of this financial instrument. The project will also be able to disseminate its results through the dissemination of its solid track record, follow-up and evaluation of the interventions and measure the impact of these on the production and well-being of

The document discusses a project aimed at implementing a low-carbon agriculture standard as part of the MADR initiative to foster sustainable development and climate resilience in rural areas. The project focuses on scaling up successful practices from pilot projects across different production chains, with support from national governments. It aims to enhance agricultural productivity while reducing greenhouse gas emissions.

The project will engage various stakeholders including producers, agro-inputs providers, financial institutions, and research organizations. It seeks to design, generate, and validate replicable success models and an escalation strategy that ensures the adoption of these practices by farmers. The project emphasizes evidence-based investment in technology packages based on lessons learned from pilot projects.

The document highlights several key contributions:
- Strengthening of agro-informatic systems will create a conducive environment for the use and production of climate-smart agricultural technologies.
- It contributes to regulatory frameworks and policies, particularly through the Agricultural Sector Policy Committee (PICGCS-Agropuertos) of MADR, which identifies, evaluates, and guides the incorporation of new technologies into national policies.
- The project aligns with the strategic line of "rural development resilient to climate change" in the National Development Plan 2018–2022.

The document also mentions the importance of the Climate Change Policy Action Plan (PACT) for Sustainable Production, which aims to achieve objectives related to poverty reduction and environmental conservation. It references the National Climate Change Policy and its alignment with the goals of the National Determined Contributions (NDCs).

Finally, it outlines economic benefits such as increased income at the local level, positive impacts on economic development and poverty reduction, reduced food insecurity, and lower costs for government insurance premium payments. Improved productivity will lead to better market access through value chains, creating more job opportunities and increasing sustainability.

The document concludes with an economic analysis of the project's potential impact (Annex 3).

### GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 48 OF 75

#### D

**Benefits of interest for both producers and consumers as a result of the adoption of technologies that increase productivity and reduce production costs, as well as the economic benefits from reduced CO₂ emissions:**

- **On-farm climate change adaptation:** The project will address the impacts of climate change on agriculture by promoting practices that are more resilient to climate change.
- **Promotion of sustainable land management:** The project will promote sustainable land management practices that reduce soil erosion and improve soil fertility, which in turn increases crop yields and reduces production costs. These practices include the use of cover crops, crop rotation, and conservation tillage.
- **Increase in the diversity of new crops and development of improved crops:** The project will support the development and promotion of new crops and varieties that are more resilient to climate change and have higher yields than traditional crops. This will increase food security and reduce the need to expand agricultural frontiers due to climate change.
- **Improvement in the planning of the agricultural sector to avoid the impacts of climatic events affecting the final production:** The project will support the development of tools and methods for better planning and management of agriculture, which will help farmers adapt their practices to changing weather patterns and reduce the risk of crop failure due to climate change.
- **Greater efficiency in public-private action, by strengthening alliances for the creation of public goods:** The project will promote collaboration between government agencies, NGOs, and private sector actors to create public goods that benefit all stakeholders. This includes the development of tools and methods for better planning and management of agriculture, which will help farmers adapt their practices to changing weather patterns.
- **Better access to information on climate change impacts and adaptation strategies:** The project will provide farmers with access to up-to-date information on climate change impacts and adaptation strategies through a variety of channels, including mobile apps, social media, and face-to-face training sessions. This will help farmers make informed decisions about their farming practices.
- **Improved availability of food, improving access and its quality:** In the event of natural disaster, the affected regions will be less vulnerable to food insecurity due to better preparedness and response mechanisms. The project will support the development of tools and methods for better planning and management of agriculture, which will help farmers adapt their practices to changing weather events.
- **Health and safety will increase:** Social inclusion will be sought, with emphasis on strengthening the role of women in agricultural production and decision-making processes.

**Emerging challenges associated with the environmental problems associated with the expansion of the agricultural frontier, other problems such as the use of chemical products, deforestation, fires, conventional tillage, lack of water resources, soil erosion, among others.**

- **Soil erosion:** 2% of the country's surface is in a severe and very severe degree of erosion.
- **Chemical inputs:** The project will support the implementation of measures that reduce the vulnerability of production systems to weather-related events, since their use increases the risk of soil degradation and loss of biodiversity. This includes promoting organic farming (native or autochthonous), making less use of chemical inputs and apply soil and water conservation practices.
- **Water resources:** The project will support the implementation of measures that reduce the vulnerability of production systems to weather-related events, since their use increases the risk of soil degradation and loss of biodiversity. This includes promoting organic farming (native or autochthonous), making less use of chemical inputs and apply soil and water conservation practices.

**Gender sensitive development impact:** In Colombia, 5.3 million women inhabit the Colombian countryside (INECO, 2018). Women are the main producers in agriculture (BEAM, PMUD, MADES, DNE, CANCELLERÍA, 2017). 79% of rural households are headed by women, and 36.6% of the agricultural workforce is female. The project will support the implementation of measures that reduce the vulnerability of production systems to weather-related events, since their use increases the risk of soil degradation and loss of biodiversity.

**D.4 Needs of recipient (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)**

#### 16. Vulnerability of Colombia and the beneficiary groups: Poverty levels are higher in rural areas of the country. In 2017, the multidimensional poverty rates in the populated and dispersed rural areas was 3.2 times higher than that in urban areas (INECO, 2018). The population living below the poverty line is also higher in dispersed rural areas (56% in the cities) compared to the rural areas (47%). In addition, there are also higher disparities between the sexes: women and children are more vulnerable. According to the INECO, 39% of all children live in households with a monthly income below the minimum wage.

**Alignment with alternative sources of financing**: As mentioned in section B.5, the nature of the initial investments that are required are of a public nature, so that domestic markets and the private sector in Colombia do not have the capacity to generate these technologies. That is why, although there may be opportunities for co-investments in investments, they do not have the financial capacity to generate the required technologies. This is the reason why the Government of Colombia that is borrowing to achieve this change. In this case, the Government of Colombia seeks a concessional loan from the Green Climate Fund (GCF) to finance the initial investments and to provide the most acceptable levels that catalyze benefits by the private sector.

#### B.5 Country ownership (max. 300 words, approximately 1 page)

**Alignment with policies and strategies at the national and local level**: There is a need to strengthen institutions at the national and local level (Agrofarms, farmer associations, etc.) for scaling up practices and technologies that are more sustainable in agriculture and rural development. The project will contribute to the strengthening of these institutions in those technologies. In this case, and as seen in the barrier analysis (section B.2) under the three principal barriers, it is necessary to strengthen the institutional framework at national level where the private sector will be strengthened. The focus on institutional strengthening is reflected in the budget, where 37 percent of the total project cost corresponds to institutional strengthening activities. These activities are designed for its implementation and will support the gremios and other local institutions after the project is completed. In a sense, these projects will contribute to the development of the private sector and the public sector, which in turn capacity will strengthen the institutions that they work with, ensuring the sustainability of the project outcomes beyond (proper) completion.

**Alignment with policies and strategies at the national level**: The GCF Country Strategy for Colombia is aligned with the National Development Plan for Equity**, in its goal for Agriculture and Rural Development to increase the area with prioritized agricultural production systems. This will be achieved through the implementation of the Policy for Comprehensive Management of Agricultural Risks and the Information System for Risk Management (SIRMA). The project also aligns with the National Strategy for Entrepreneurship and Productivity aims to encourage investments in the field through the reform of the incentives system, which includes the creation of a new agricultural credit line. In addition, it is aligned with the Sustainability "producing conserving and preserving conservation" that seeks a balance between the conservation of natural resources (water, forests, soil, biodiversity) and the productive use of these resources: materials, energy and soil. It also promotes the development of the circular economy, through innovation and the adoption of new technologies in agriculture and rural development. The project is aligned with the national strategies that are intended to prevent and reduce disaster risk or achieve adaptation to climate change, for which it will contribute to the implementation of the National Plan for Adaptation to Climate Change (PNACC).

**Alignment with policies and strategies at the local level**: Likewise, it is aligned with the various climate change plans and strategies such as the National Determined Contributions (NDCs) of Colombia, the National Strategy for Entrepreneurship and Productivity, the National Plan for Adaptation to Climate Change (PNACC), and the prioritized actions of "agriculture production and food security" in the National Strategic Plan for Sustainable Development (PNSD). The project is also affected by temperature change and precipitation, as well as by floods, droughts, soil fertility. It trains and disseminates knowledge on climate change adaptation strategies to farmers and other actors involved in the agricultural value chain and implements adaptation actions in climate-smart agriculture; Recover productive capacity of degraded soils and improve water use efficiency; Invest in technology and innovation for climate-smart agriculture; Similarly, it is aligned with the Green Growth Policy (2018), under the objective of strengthening the mechanisms that promote the development of a green economy. The project will contribute to the implementation of the Colombian Strategy for Low Carbon Development (ECONIC), which includes as objectives: i) Empower and motivate farmers in the adoption of climate-smart agriculture practices; ii) Promote the achievement of growth goals and generating social benefits - economic and environmental; iii) Develop mitigation actions plan in

127. **Role of National Designated Authority (NDA)** In Colombia, the National Planning Department (DNP) is the designated National Authority (NDA), which supports its decision-making in a collegiate Body made up of the DNP, the Ministry of Environment and Sustainable Development (MADS), the Ministry of Foreign Affairs (MINEX), the Presidency Agency for International Cooperation and the DNP, which exercise their functions within the framework of the GCF. The NDA is responsible for developing strategies to promote the mobilization of financing resources from the GCF in accordance with its result areas, generating the tools and mechanisms necessary for the implementation of the project, ensuring that the project activities undergo a review by the Collegiate Body and has incorporated the comments provided. This process has verified the alignment between the project’s objectives, strategies and goals with those of the National Climate Change Strategy and the Green Growth Policy, and the Predicted and Developed Development Scenarios (PDS) and the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), as well as other criteria aligned with the investment criteria of the GCF.

A total of 24 group work sessions were held with 86 officials from 14 national institutions and 3 international organizations. The results of these meetings have been incorporated into the project document.

### D. Efficiency and Effectiveness

#### 8.5. Financial Structure
As mentioned in Box B, the project will have a combination of loans (assumed by the Government of Colombia) for an amount of USD 33.3 million. On the other hand, the local counterpart amounts to USD 10.7 million.

The GCF financing is expected to cover approximately 48% of the total cost of the Project. That is, 48.3% of the total amount of the project are grants, minus the concessionality of the GCF funds. GCF financing will be used for the implementation of the CGIAR Project and the technical assistance for the Project. On the other hand, it is expected that with the Project, and in addition to the co-financing of the farmer associations, there will be a greater participation of private investment in the project, which will come from the private sector; once there is a greater adoption of the practices and technologies developed and validated by the project.

#### 8.6. Efficiency and Effectiveness
The ex-ante economic analysis for the implementation of the CGIAP project shows that the project has an internal rate of return (IRR) of around 19%, which results from the high impact of climate smart technologies available at scale for producers and generating high returns at the producer level. It is worth noting that the use of these technologies is not only economically viable, but also socially sustainable because they are reimbursable instruments to the producers (instead of grants) as are not viable because the kind of investments are usually very large and do not have a return on investment in the short term.

The project has been evaluated with respect to the criteria for selection of projects that are available in Colombia for the type of investment (gene banks and seed improvement, big data, GHG emissions reduction, etc.). The project meets all these criteria. In addition, it is estimated that around 10 percent of total project costs go to public infrastructure that do not have direct revenue generation potential.

The GCF financing offers a unique opportunity for blending low interest loans and grants, reducing the financial cost of the project, and representing a specialized source, especially designed to enhance climate resilience and


---

### Best available technologies and practices

The project will make use of best practices and innovative technologies to promote sustainable agricultural development in the agricultural sector. Specifically, and as described in section B.3, a Big Data platform will be developed and operated for the collection, analysis, and dissemination of information on climate change impacts and adaptation measures. The delivery of recommendations for adaptation and mitigation measures, to producers in a reliable, timely and site-specific manner, is also planned (see section B.4). This will be achieved through the use of the following technologies: 103 for the project, varying between crops between 17.6 and 219.5, depending on the level of adoption.

### Best available technologies and practices

The project will make use of best practices and innovative technologies to promote sustainable agricultural development in the agricultural sector. Specifically, and as described in section B.3, a Big Data platform will be developed and operated for the collection, analysis, and dissemination of information on climate change impacts and adaptation measures. The delivery of recommendations for adaptation and mitigation measures, to producers in a reliable, timely and site-specific manner, is also planned (see section B.4). This will be achieved through the use of the following technologies: 103 for the project, varying between crops between 17.6 and 219.5, depending on the level of adoption.

## E.1. Paradigm shift objectives

### Shift to low-emission sustainable development pathways
- Increased climate resilient sustainable development

## E.2. Core indicator targets

Provide specific numerical values for the GCF core indicators to be achieved by the project/programme. These should be provided in the annexes and should be consistent with the information provided in the project/programme proposal.

### E.2.1. Expected tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (t CO₂ eq) to be reduced (mitigation and cross-cutting only)
- Annual: 368,081 t CO₂ eq
- Lifetime: 9,152,034 t CO₂ eq

### E.2.2. Estimated cost per t CO₂ eq, defined as total investment costs divided by the estimated emissions reductions (mitigation and cross-cutting only)
- Total cost: $89,915,201 USD
- Requested GCF amount: $73,283,080 USD
- Expected lifetime emission reductions: 9,152,034 t CO₂ eq
- Estimated cost per t CO₂ eq (d / a + c): $10.9 USD/tCO₂eq
- Estimated cost per t CO₂ eq (b / d + e): $0.01 USD/tCO₂eq

### E.2.3. Expected volume of finance leveraged by the proposed project/programme as a ratio of public and private sources, disaggregated by public and private financing (mitigation and cross-cutting only)
- Total finance leveraged: $86,727,121 USD
- Public finance leveraged: $13,905,421 USD
- Private source finance leveraged: $18,327,231 USD
- Total leverage ratio (d / g + h): 0.36
- Public source co-financing ratio (g / d + e): 0.14
- Private source co-financing ratio (h / d + f): 0.22

### E.2.4. Expected total number of direct and indirect beneficiaries, disaggregated by sex
- Direct: 619,691
    - Male: 375,841
    - Female: 243,850 (20% female)
- Indirect: 347,996

### E.2.5. Number of beneficiaries relative to total population in Colombia (disaggregated by sex)
- Direct: 1.21% (Expressed as %) of total population in Colombia
    - Male: 0.8%
    - Female: 1.7%
- Indirect: 0.68% (Expressed as %) of total population in Colombia
    - Male: 0.5%
    - Female: 1.2%

It is important to note that approximately 13% of the GCF contribution is considered within the mitigation results area (see annex A). a) Total investment costs are calculated based on the project/programme proposal and include all direct and indirect costs, including those related to the implementation of the project/programme. b) The total leverage ratio is calculated as the sum of public and private sources divided by the total cost. c) The public source co-financing ratio is calculated as the sum of public sources divided by the total cost plus the sum of public sources. d) The private source co-financing ratio is calculated as the sum of private sources divided by the total cost plus the sum of private sources.

See Annex D1 and Annex A24 for further details regarding the calculations and assumptions for direct and indirect beneficiaries.

#### E.7 Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements (max. 500 words; approximately 1 page)

**134. Monitoring and evaluation systems:**

The monitoring and evaluation system for the impact of the CiSACP Project will consider the different levels (producer, farmer associations and other relevant actors) that will be participating in the implementation of the project. The indicators established by the Monitoring Plan for each component will be specific indicators from farmers' association will also be added. To carry out the implementation of the Monitoring system, a monitoring plan will be developed with the participation of all stakeholders involved.

The monitoring and evaluation systems will be implemented by the CIAT Bovetalliance, Inc., by the Projects, Applied Economics and Impact team (if applicable), and by the project partners. The monitoring and evaluation system will include primary data sources and conducting periodic monitoring analyses. In methodological terms, the appropriate methods will be used to collect information from different levels of actors involved in the project.

The monitoring plan will be complemented with qualitative analyzes. In the first phase, a baseline will be designed and built; taking advantage of existing databases and indicators, as well as other relevant data sources that are available for each component. The baseline will be developed so that the final impact of the Project can be measured.

**135. Indicators:**

The indicators to measure the impact on water resources and low carbon (emission reduction and carbon capture), the water footprint and the carbon footprint of the proposed systems will be necessary to evaluate the performance of the project in terms of its environmental objectives. The monitoring system so that the comparative advantages of the proposed systems can be demonstrated. The recommendation document will include a list of indicators for each component, which will be used as a basis for the selection and implemented that will be the creation of technology transfer models and other technical support to producers who adopt these technologies.

**136. Monitoring by CIAT:**

The Unit will carry out administrative missions or inspection visits, depending on the importance of the indicators established in each component. The monitoring system will be coordinated among the different components of the project through a joint meeting between the Project Manager and the Unit to the Cap, to discuss, update and review the progress of the Project's monitoring tools, along with other aspects related to the implementation of the project. This will allow the Unit to monitor the performance against the indicators established in the Monitoring Plan for each component (e.g., Annual Operations Report for the following year), the status of the project (e.g., budget and financial management), the progress of the project's progress, (v) update of the Risk Matrix, and (vi) possible modifications to the budget allocations by component. The Unit will also be responsible for preparing a report on the monitoring system that will include an analysis of which will be used to develop the reports and data submitted to the Cap. These aspects will be presented at the periodic meetings between the Project Manager and the Unit.

**137. Reports:**

During project implementation, the monitoring strategy will be applied with the following reporting mechanisms:

- **Annual reports:** The annual report will provide an overview of the project's progress.
- **Semianual reports:** The semianual reports will allow feedback on the different activities and make recommendations for the continuation of the project in progress. These reports will include indicators from the sources mentioned above and with a guide of indicators already established. These reports will have

# F. Risk Assessment and Management

## F.1 Risk Factors and Mitigation Measures (max: 3 pages)

### Selected Risk Factor 1
**Category:** Technical and operational  
**Probability:** Medium  
**Impact:** Medium  

#### Description
Stakeholder risk in general, the community of farmers and ranchers tends to be very conservative when adapting to new technologies, varieties or crops, presenting a medium risk but positive resistance to the introduction of new profitable products or systems.

#### Mitigation Measure(s)
This risk can be mitigated by establishing close relationships with relevant stakeholders, including the Government and stakeholders at the end of the value chain. These measures decrease the probability that the risk will occur, to a low level.

### Selected Risk Factor 2
**Category:** Technical and operational  
**Probability:** Low  
**Impact:** Medium  

#### Description
Technical performance risk: This risk is related to possible incorrect design or management of trials and pilot projects, or failures in partnership agreements or institutional settings. The probability of these risks occurring is very low due to the experience of the team only representing.

#### Mitigation Measure(s)
To mitigate this risk, G4AT will establish a Project Coordination Team with the capacity to travel to monitor progress and detect mismanagement and non-compliance risks early, and take action on time.

### Selected Risk Factor 3
**Category:** Technical and operational  
**Probability:** Medium  
**Impact:** Medium  

#### Description
Risk of delay and cancellation of costs: There is an inherent risk of delay in planned scheduled works, including the acquisition of equipment and materials. This risk is likely to occur with a medium impact. In the event of a time delay, a possible result is increased costs.

#### Mitigation Measure(s)
Contingencies should be included in the project budget as a mitigation measure for this risk. Another measure is a close budgetary and financial monitoring to avoid possible delays or cancellations. A CIAT coordinator will be in charge of the operational aspects to detect early risks and act on time.

### Selected Risk Factor 4
**Category:** Governance  
**Probability:** Medium  
**Impact:** Medium  

#### Description
Sustainability risk: The lack of governance or mechanisms that limit the sustainability of the project is a mix of medium probability and high impact.


---
**Page 69**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 PAGE 75 OF 75

## Mitigation Measure(s)

From the beginning of the project, each actor will have clear responsibilities assigned during the execution period, as well as after the project ends and will be assumed by the farmers themselves. Strengthening producer associations will help to ensure that the project is financially sustainable when it is completed. Maintenance and operation plans will be established to provide adequate maintenance to the infrastructure and equipment over its life.

### Selected Risk Factor 5

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Low | Medium |

#### Description

Location risk: The project will carry out activities in remote areas with difficult access, which represents a risk for the execution of the project in terms of delays and costs.

### Mitigation Measure(s)

To mitigate this risk, the location will be a criterion for the selection of pilots, and those closest to the logistics structure will be preferred. CIAT will have the capacity to provide supervision at all implementation sites, with regular visits and monitoring.

## Selected Risk Factor 6

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Forex | Medium | Medium |

#### Description

Foreign exchange risk: Financial planning can be affected by currency fluctuations in the future.

### Mitigation Measure(s)

Coverage and good planning can help mitigate this risk. This would not change the probability, but it would mitigate the impact that the exchange rate may have on project costs.

## Selected Risk Factor 7

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| ML/FT | Low | High |

#### Description

Probability and impact for money laundering, terrorist financing and prohibited practices risks.

CAF has established an AML program, customer screening, monitoring and related due diligence procedures that are designed to prevent doing business with entities and/or persons who engage in illegal activities, sanctions violations or other prohibited practices. CAF is a member of the Global Fintech Alliance (GFA), which includes the US - OFAC, UK, France, Canada, World Bank, Inter-American Development Bank, and a designated Compliance Officer.

The contract (legal agreement) signed among CAF and executive agency and other relevant parties, there are obligations to comply with AML and FT regulations and to prevent prohibited practices. Additionally, the business manager performs due diligence through the AML/CFT risk profile and KYC checklist. They also carry out the screening of counterparties and related parties. The information on money laundering and terrorist financing is updated according to the risk profile, once a year for low-risk clients and twice a year for medium risk.

## Volatility of agricultural commodities prices.

### Mitigation Measure(s)
As part of its implementation, the CSICAP project will contribute to the reduction of crop-price volatility, often associated with price shocks, with the introduction of appropriate services and improved risk management, which will allow farmers to improve decision making, reducing uncertainty and better coupling production with market demand. The project will also provide a buffer stock for the project itself in case of low prices or high prices of inputs. It is possible to reach an agreement with the producers' association (which are strategic partners of the project) who have this stock and the quantity required by the project is quite small.

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Low | Medium |

Description:
There is a pool of human talent that has worked in previous/similar experiences as the CSICAP project in Colombia so the risk to find suitable contractors to undertake the work at the expected price point is low. The risk will be mitigated during the formulation process of the CSICAP project with the farmers associations (gremios) and the MADR, the transfer of best practices from previous experiences and the lessons learned from the pilot. As a result, there are dissemination and training activities with the technicians and with farmers' association members to ensure that they have all the necessary information for the implementation of the project. The strategic partners for the implementation, so their human talent skills have been considered in the whole process.

# GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS

## C1. Environmental and social risk assessment (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

### Provide the environmental and social risk category

#### 142. Environmental and Social Risk Category: Taking into account the GCF safeguards evaluation exercise and the Project Baseline Report, the project is categorized in Category B, that it, presents moderate environmental and social impacts in the project's intervention areas. The project will specifically intervene in areas where there are existing or potential conflicts between agricultural activities and conservation of natural ecosystems through the implementation of good agricultural practices such as reducing the water and carbon footprint, as well as improving soil fertility and biodiversity. The identification of environmental and social risks was carried out in which it is evaluated how these risks can be mitigated by the project's measures and how they will be managed during and after the project. The project will also contribute to the reduction of poverty through the proposed mitigation measures, especially in relation to the inclusion of women in the activities and benefits of the project.

#### 143. Environmental and Social Risk Assessment (ESIA)

The environmental and social risk assessment (ESIA) was carried out by the project team for this project, for CICISP, in line with the requirements determined by the GCF for a project Category B in Paramount ecosystems, according to the ESIA guidelines of the GCF. The ESIA is based on the information provided by the communities and especially when it comes to livestock activities, as well as measures to reduce the use of agricultural chemicals. In addition, it is all part of the project's mitigation strategy that will be implemented during its implementation.

The ESIA also identifies the risks associated with the project in terms of the environmental and social impacts, in which the existing challenges are addressed in the implementation of agricultural practices in mountain ecosystems. The ESIA also includes a risk assessment for the communities involved in the project, including the government, especially the MDRP to document and implement sustainable production practices with low water footprint and carbon footprint.

The ESIA is based on the information provided by the communities and especially when it comes to livestock activities, as well as measures to reduce the use of agricultural chemicals. In addition, it is all part of the project's mitigation strategy that will be implemented during its implementation.

#### 144. The social analysis showed that most of the products in the chains and territories prioritized by the project are produced for local consumption or for sale within the territory. This is due to the low connectivity of these territories, which makes it difficult for producers to access markets outside their communities. In addition, there is a lack of participation, but also in relation to the promotion of change, the adoption of knowledge and new technologies, and the commercialization of products. The ESIA also identifies the risks associated with the project in terms of environmental and social impacts.

The ESIA also includes a risk assessment for the communities involved in the project, including the government, especially the MDRP to document and implement sustainable production practices with low water footprint and carbon footprint.

#### 145. Given this scenario, the ESMF recommends, among other aspects, a more participatory approach, in which a specific group of producers is identified for each chain or territory prioritized by the project. This group will respond, in a differentiated way, to the specific characteristics of the products of each production chain and the conditions that prevail in each territory.

The ESIA also includes a risk assessment for the communities involved in the project, including the government, especially the MDRP to document and implement sustainable production practices with low water footprint and carbon footprint.

#### 146. In conclusion, the ESIA identifies the risks associated with the project in terms of environmental and social impacts. The following plans and strategies:

- Risk management plan associated with security issues.
- Communication plan.
- Risk management plan for natural disasters and environmental emergencies.
- Comprehensive solid waste management plan and development and implementation of roadmaps.
- Environmental, Social and Gender Action Plan (ESGAP) for Environmental Services restoration, landscape management tools, conservation agreements, among others.
- Updating the occupational health and safety plans, measures and protocols related to the use of pesticides and agrochemicals.

#### 147. Regarding the administration of the ESMP, it was defined that the Project Management Unit will be in charge of the implementation of the ESMF recommendations. The Project Management Unit is composed by farmer associations, Agrosavia and other entities that carry out project activities. The Unit will have an Environmental, Social and Gender Team (ESGT) to ensure compliance with the ESMP. The ESGT will be led by the project who will be its leader. The Project Management Unit, through the Environmental, Social and Gender Team, will provide guidelines and technical advice for compliance with the ESMP. The Environmental, Social and

Gender Team will be in charge of designing ESMP guidelines, plans and strategies, providing support during implementation, monitoring progress and compliance as stipulated in ESMP and supporting the regular updating of ESMP if, in any case, it is required, it is possible for the stakeholder's access to the GCF’s Independent System (Independent System).

### 149. Capacity of the concerned parties to implement the ESMP

In summary, two groups of farmer associations are involved: the PCCSD and the CAC. The PCCSD has a management plan, strengthening its information systems and monitoring of affiliates and environmental issues. The CAC is in charge of the project implementation and follows the ESMP guidelines and the following the Green Climate Fund (GCF) Action Plan (during funding and after implementation). Those responsible for each activity were established (CAC, PCCSD, GCF, DAT, TNC, Fondo de Desarrollo Intersindical). The methods and reporting procedures are defined and reporting is regular.

### 150. Project Implementation

The project will be implemented by the CAC as the representative of the PCCSD and the Peruvian government. Claims, Suggestions and Denunciations (PCSD), the following measures are considered:

- **Claiming**: The claimant must be a member of the PCCSD or an individual who has signed up for the project; which will include a section for registering the PCSD that may be submitted. Users will be able to directly submit the PCSD through the CAC website and the GCF website.
- **Document management**: Each PCSD will have a file number. The PCSD can also be carried out directly through the CAC website or the GCF website. The person in charge of document management of the ESMP team will register in the system.

### 151. The PCCSD Resolution Mechanism

The resolution mechanism will be formalized and structured for processing and response. In the case of communications and PCSD referred by women and ethnic groups, they will be addressed to the Gender Team. The Gender Team will be responsible for the communication strategy and response in the case of communications and PCSD referred by women and ethnic groups.

### 152. PCCSD Resolution Mechanism

The resolution mechanism includes a classification of the complaint according to its level of urgency, gravity, and complexity. The classification is based on the type of claim, the number of affected people, and risk. According to the classification, the official/contractor who has the competence to attend it will be reported immediately. The response time for each category will be established in the ESMP. The Gender Team will be responsible for the response to the claim will be given, specifying the actions and the person/s responsible for carrying them out. The resolution of the complaint will be recorded in the system and a report on the progress of the resolution will be provided. Each PCSD will be followed up and formally closed.

### 153. Regular Consultations

Regular consultations with stakeholders during project implementation were carried out, taking into account that the project is still in the structure stage and that at the time of the consultation, the formulation of the ESMP was not yet completed. The CAC has been working on the ESMP since its inception. The development of ESMP was made subsequently inspired about the main strengths, possible obstacles and opportunities of the project. The CAC has been working with stakeholders to ensure that their concerns are addressed between actors, and to guarantee success in the inclusion of a gender approach and environmental management strategy.

### 154. To maintain interaction with the different stakeholders during project implementation

A stakeholder consultation plan was established:

- **Start-up workshop**
- **Strengthening CAC and ESMP that include participatory exercises (strategy of social appropriation of knowledge, work tables and spaces for discussion and collective construction of knowledge with producers, participation in workshops, meetings, seminars, etc.)**
- **Communication strategy**: The plan includes the selection of beneficiaries, celebration of events with rights and commitments of the beneficiaries, participation of beneficiaries in decision-making bodies of the project (CAC, PCCSD, GCF, DAT, TNC, Fondo de Desarrollo Intersindical)
- **Potential impacts on indigenous populations**: The CAC/PCSD project will not have a direct impact on collective territories. However, it is important to consider that the project has been designed with an ethnic differential approach and prior consultation processes. However, with the aim of promoting the participation of ethnic producers in the project, the ethnic differential approach was included in the diagnosis and analysis of risks, as well as in the mitigation

measures and in the dissemination and relationship processes that the Project will implement. CSICAP: Specifics are considered pertinent to take the following provisions:

- Include the participation of producers belonging to ethnic communities in the group of extension workers, people belonging to ethnic communities in the project intervention areas, in such a way that trust is generated, work with the communities is strengthened, and their knowledge is recognized.

## G.2: Gender assessment and action plan (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)


---
**Page 74**
---

### Development of the Gender Analysis and Gender Action Plan

The Gender Analysis comes out a comprehensive diagnosis with a gender perspective of the territories, the population and the agricultural value chains that are the focus of the GCSAP project, as well as the institutional, normative and policy framework associated to them. The analysis is based on triangulation of secondary and statistical information (population census, agricultural census, and quality of life indicators), qualitative data from interviews with key informants, participatory rural appraisal (PRA) exercises, associations of an initial gender equality analysis forms, as well as the development of interviews and group sessions with farmers and women’s organizations. The results are presented in a Gender Analysis Report.

For the formulation of the Gender Action Plan, the gender approach was analyzed and mainstreamed in each of the four components of the project (see Annex 1). In this regard, the following were identified: i) The Gender Analysis, the risks associated with gender were identified and, on the one hand, mitigation measures were formulated to reduce these risks; ii) On the other hand, measures to promote gender equality in order for the project to contribute to reducing these gaps.

### Barriers to women’s participation

Women face a number of barriers that prevent them from participating fully in agricultural production in rural contexts. Consequently, household and unpaid care work are activities that fall on young women, which can lead to their exclusion from productive activities. Women also have limited access to land (particularly in the case of women-headed households), credit, inputs, extension services, and markets. This is a major challenge for the project’s economic development.

### Gender Action Plan

In its Gender Action Plan, the GCSAP project seeks to reduce these barriers faced by rural women who participate who produce who will take advantage of it to identify opportunities, in an articulated manner, with the objective of promoting their participation and empowerment. In relation to the care economy* and women’s rights, actions will be based developed on the 3R concept: i) Recognition (of women’s work); ii) Redistribution (of tasks between men and women, including extension workers). iii) Reduction (of time that women dedicate to these activities, facilitating access to care services and improving their quality of life).

Redistribution of loads in the home (training to producers and agency promotion, participation and empowerment of women in decision-making processes, etc.) will be a priority. The objective is for rural women to become more productive, flexible workers, comprehensive assistance, “producer to producer” methodology, access to credit, land tenure security, etc.

Likewise, a greater participation of women in the Project will be fostered by, taking advantage of economic opportunities and the creation of new markets (e.g., through the development of value chains for specific products), situational agroforestry, which in turn can improve household food security. Socializing the project’s gender perspective is also an important objective to ensure that women fulfill their mission, so that in this way they have the necessary tools to implement the action plan.

### Gender Action Plan Implementation

Several of these actions will be implemented through a “bottom-up” approach. Several of the actions will be implemented not only with the direct beneficiaries (women producers), but also with other actors involved in the project, such as extension workers and women’s organizations. These suggestions were incorporated into the actions proposed in the GAP, several of which are being implemented by the GCSAP project team.

The Gender Action Plan is a living document that will be updated on an ongoing basis to ensure its relevance and effectiveness. The plan will be reviewed at least once per year. It will be promoted by a senior and a junior professional, who will be in charge of the design of guidelines, plans and strategies of the GAP, provide support and technical advice during implementation, monitor progress and compliance as stipulated in the GAP, and support the review and update of the plan.

*In CEMAC countries, on average, more than two production losses in 40% of UPA are caused by 20% of women who make decisions.
**Article 2 [of Law No. 3/2010] defines access to economic assets as “overall control over the home is related to the maintenance of the case, the care of children and other family members, the management of household resources, and the decision-making on the use of these resources.”

## Financial Management and Control

The MADR, as Executive Entity, will be responsible for supervising the implementation and supervision of the project in accordance with its own internal rules, policies and procedures, including financial management and control.

The CAF will provide technical assistance to the MADR in the development of these rules, policies and procedures. The CAF will also provide training on financial management and reporting responsibilities. The loan resources will be administered according to the CAF Credit Process Manual (MPM) and the CAF Procurement Manual (PM). The MIP will be responsible for supervising the process for all phases of credit, including: (i) Origination; (ii) Evaluation; (iii) Approval; (iv) Formalization; and (v) Administration. The MADR will be responsible for implementing the project in accordance with the CAF Credit Process Manual (MPM), through MADR as the Executive Agency, and GAT, as the procured party. The figure in this section shows the execution scheme of the GCF funds and how these would be handled by GAT as an executing agency.

## Figure G.3.1: Flow chart, management and financial control of the Project

The financial and procurement processes will be implemented in accordance with the rules and regulations of the CAF, which were certified in the accreditation process before the GCF. On the other hand, CIAT has all the necessary tools to implement its own internal rules, policies and procedures. CIAT has a logistics agreement and budget execution online, it also has logistics and travel modules that allow requests to be made for goods and services, as well as the possibility of requesting the signature of the CAF representative for approval workflows. CIAT handles the accrual accounting established in the procedures documented in Directive 013.5. The financial control is carried out by the Financial Management Unit (FMU), which will monitor the CGIAR internal audit and, on occasions, the audit of specific projects that are carried out by external auditors. The FMU also monitors the execution of the project according to the terms of the signed agreements with the budget availability within the agreement.

CIAT will be responsible for implementing its own rules, policies and procedures in accordance with its policies and procedures. This may include procurement, risk checks, and participation in Steering Committee meetings. The project management unit (PMU) is linked to the CAF through the MADR as the executing agency. CIAT is responsible for financial monitoring and will receive support from a financial management team, which will interact directly with the FMU.

Dear Madam, Sir,

We refer to the project titled “Climate-smart initiatives for climate change adaptation and sustainability in prioritized agricultural production systems in Colombia” (C3S-CAP) - in Colombia - concerning the funding proposal submitted by Corporación Andina de Fomento (CAF) to us on April 30th, 2021.

The undersigned is the duly authorized representative of the National Planning Department of Colombia.

Pursuant to GCF decision B.06/19, the content of which we acknowledge to have reviewed, we hereby communicate our no-objection to the project as included in the funding proposal.

By communicating our no-objection, it is implied that:

(a) The government of Colombia has no objection to the project as included in the funding proposal;

(b) The project as included in the funding proposal is in conformity with the national priorities, strategic objectives and policies; and

(c) In accordance with the GCF’s environmental and social safeguards, the project as included in the funding proposal will not have a significant adverse impact on important natural sites or ecosystems.

We also confirm that our national process for providing no-objection to the project as included in the funding proposal has been duly followed.

# Secretariat's assessment of FP182

## Overall assessment of the Secretariat

The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks:

| Strengths | Points of caution |
| --- | --- |
| Modernization of advisory extension services and adaptation decision-making at the producer level with cutting-edge technologies, including artificial intelligence. | Relatively high costs of hardware and technicians and a need to ensure that funding is used to build the capacity of local actors. |
| Great potential to attract the private sector in the targeted agricultural value chains (right crops and locations). | |
| Innovative solutions already in place with high demands from local, national and international actors. | |
| High potential for further scaling up and replication to other geocrop (farmer producer groups) and agroecological models which have been developed and proven to be successful through pilot projects. | |

The Board may wish to consider approving this funding proposal with the terms and conditions listed in the respective term sheet and addendum V, titled "List of proposed conditions and recommendations."

## Summary of the Secretariat's assessment

### 2.1 Project background

The agricultural sector in Colombia has been negatively affected by climate change. Its production, yield, land suitability and harvest seasons have all been impacted by higher temperatures, rainfall variability, droughts and a higher frequency of extreme weather events.


---
**Page 83**
---

4. The objectives of the project are to change the current paradigm of agricultural production by extensive use of conventional agricultural inputs with little adaptation techniques, and to reduce the vulnerability of crops to droughts, floods and other climatic stresses through adopting digital agriculture, which includes the use of big data and artificial intelligence technologies. The project will also contribute to ensuring food security and stable availability of good-quality food by strengthening climate risk management while reducing greenhouse gas emissions (GHG). The project is expected to strengthen the capacity of producers, technicians and institutions so that they can adopt and implement digital agriculture in Colombia. The project will be implemented on eight crops - rice, corn, potatoes, sugarcane, panela sugarcane, coffee, bananas and plantains.

5. The project will contribute directly benefiting 619,611 rural producers and their families (80 per cent of the country's agricultural population) and indirectly benefitting 23 million people through the provision of climate information services. It is also expected to increase the production of coffee by 47% in Colombia. The project will train a total of 5,961 technicians, distributed in 22 departments (60 per cent of the country's departments) and 219 municipalities (20 per cent of all municipalities). The project is projected to reduce GHG emissions by 347,800 tonnes of CO2 equivalent over a period of 5 years with an average lifespan.

6. The total project financing is USD 99,919,201, with a request for GCF financing of USD 73,283,080 (grant USD 48,283,080 and loan USD 25,000,000). Corporación Andina de Fomento (CAF) contributes to the project with a senior loan of USD 10,300,000, and co-financing of USD 16,049,500. The project is implemented in Colombia through an alliance between Biodiversidad International and the International Center for Tropical Agriculture (CIAT), which acts as the implementing entity or "grantee". The project is submitted under environmental and social safeguards category B.

### Component-by-component analysis

#### Component 1: Digital agriculture and climate services for rural modernization with an emphasis on adaptation and mitigation (total cost USD 39,615,408; GCF cost USD 26,045,000 or 66 per cent)

- **Component 1 aims to improve decision-making at the producer level and avoid crop losses through the adoption of digital agriculture, including the use of big data and artificial intelligence technologies. The project will provide climate information services for producers and technicians in addition. This component includes early warning systems for extreme weather events, early warning systems on pests, diseases and other abiotic factors that can occur due to climatic phenomena.

- **Under this component**, GCF grants and loans will be used to strengthen and modernize the agro-climatic information system (SICA) in Colombia. The SICA is a climate risk management tool for agriculture in Colombia. Under 2,200 technicians will be trained in using this type of tool, and the component will contribute to the implementation of digital agriculture through adaptation and mitigation measures delivered to about 49,200 producers who are considered direct project beneficiaries.

- **This component** shows the key paradigm-shift potential in the proposed project by bringing in cutting-edge technologies to extension services and farming activities. Adopting artificial intelligence (AI) and big data will allow for more efficient climate risk monitoring and data management, and provide automatic responses in extension services. The focus is on using AI and big data to improve decision-making processes compared with conventional statistical methods, and provides sound justification for discarding the conventional methods and opting for the proposed solution. The target production sites are


---
**Page 84**
---

well equipped to implement the proposed solutions because such systems are not entirely new for some of the crops (e.g. sugarcane and cotton). The proposal also shows that sufficient technical capacity is in place from both CAF and Agricultural Research Corporation of Colombia (Agricor).

10. The project is structured so that minimum concessionality is sought to make the investments viable with the participation of both the private and public sector. As demonstrated in the feasibility study, the project would be unviable if the concessional funding were removed. When reducing the grant amount by a third, for instance, it would make the project unviable for certain crops such as sugarcane and cotton. The proposal also shows that the concessionality is such that the project is not viable for most of the grains. This demonstrates that the concessionality in this project is minimal and that the private sector will be able to finance a large part of it, which in turn have committed to provide co-financing so that the project's objectives are met.

Component 2: Genetic improvement, crop management techniques and other technological options and their integration into the modernized sugarcane production systems (total cost: USD 84.015 million; GCF cost: USD 37.962 million or 45 per cent)

11. Component 2 will improve the resilience of production systems and promote low-carbon agricultural practices by developing climate-resilient sugarcane varieties, including heat-tolerant new varieties and hybrids that are more resilient to climate impacts, efficient use of water resources, and nitrogen-use-efficient and low-GHG-emission varieties for rice. The component has two thematic outputs with their corresponding activities: (i) strengthening the germplasm bank, developing new varieties, and supporting the massive multiplication of seeds for adaptation and mitigation; and (ii) promoting the adoption of crop management techniques that will enable scaling up to increase resilience and mitigation.

12. Specifically, the project’s work plans will be used to develop a state-of-the-art phenotyping platform designed to identify climate-resilient genetic resources aimed at developing heat- and drought-resistant, nitrogen-use-efficient and low-GHG-emission varieties for rice and sugarcane. The phenotyping platform will be used for implementing a range of crop management techniques on 92,300 hectares (10 per cent) of the project area in order to increase yields, reduce GHG emissions or increase carbon capture, while enabling productivity increases.

13. The proposal provides sound justification for the need of a germplasm bank and development of new varieties and hybrids that are more resilient to climate impacts than alternative options assessed in the feasibility study. There are limited options available in the market in terms of heat- and drought-resistant sugarcane varieties, which will take longer time than the project period. A phenotyping platform would thus be the most cost-effective option for developing new varieties.

14. The proposed crop management techniques and technologies are well known recognized agricultural practices that will produce no regret results. These techniques will be tailored to the specific conditions of the project area and will include the tools needed for implementing these agronomic measures, including methods to monitor the GHG emissions and carbon capture. The proposal provides a detailed description of each intervention, their rationale and the steps for implementation are assessed as adequate and reasonable to achieve the project objectives.

Component 3: Development of business models through modernized irrigation systems and a more engaged financial sector (total cost: USD 157.61 million; GCF cost: USD 71.699 million, or 45 per cent)

15. Component 3 aims to develop science-based business models that will enable prioritized value chains to open new commercial opportunities. The component will also transfer modernized technical assistance and agricultural extension services to 2,200 trainees under


---
**Page 85**
---

component 1: The component is expected to generate eight business models (one per genus) and involve four financial institutions (banks, microfinance institutions, insurance companies and so on), incentivizing them to design tailored financial products for the target agricultural sector.

The business model development under this component has been strengthened through extensive reviews conducted during the second-level due diligence. The outputs of this component will be a set of business models that are expected to significantly strengthen the genomics' capacity to use adopted digital tools, and developing low-carbon and climate-resilient business models for the target agricultural sector. These business models will also create well-functioning domestic and international markets with active public and private sectors, including financial institutions. The project will be implemented in two phases: Phase 1 focuses on developing differentiated business models that integrate the technological solutions in agriculture breeding and production, providing new commercial opportunities for the private sector in the target agricultural sector.

Project management (Total cost: USD 692 million; GCF cost USD 278.0 million or 73 per cent)

The GCF portion of the project management cost is about 3.7 per cent of the total requested GCF funding and is compliant with the GCF policy on fees.

### III. Assessment of performance against investment criteria

#### 3.1 Impact potential
Scale: Medium to high

Adaptation impact is deemed to be significant; the number of direct beneficiaries is 72 per cent of the total producer population in Colombia, and the scope of the project is national, with the project's target departments accounting for close to 70 per cent of the country departmental area. The project has a high mitigation potential, and agreed among project stakeholders including genomics. Rigorous eligibility criteria have been established for the selection of projects that will be financed as part of the funding proposal package and they are included in the term sheet.

As the project scope is at the national level, the climate rationale for the adaptation intervention is based on the observed historical trends in temperature and precipitation. Colombia has been experiencing an average temperature increase of 0.17°C per decade between 1971 and 2006, and will continue to experience a further increase over the next decades due to the higher frequency of extreme weather events. The proposal lacks observed historical trends for each identified parameter (temperature, precipitation) at the project level.

While efforts have been made to identify a link between El Niño Southern Oscillation (ENSO) and agricultural productivity in Colombia, there is no evidence that can be based on the observable long-term trend between identified parameters and agricultural productivity.

#### 3.2 Paradigm shift potential
Scale: Medium


---
**Page 86**
---

## Sustainable development potential: Scale: Medium

The proposal provides sound justification for its potential to generate positive environmental and social benefits, including increased productivity, as capital gains in the framework, and increased food security will deliver health and nutrition improvements for the target farmer families. The proposal also provides other economic and social co-benefits that have been considered.

### Needs of the country: Scale: Medium

The vulnerability of the country and the target beneficiaries and their adaptation needs are generally explained throughout the proposal, but without detailed descriptions on scale and intensity of the country's exposure to climate risks. The impacts of climate change, including increased frequency and severity of extreme weather events, and its effects on livelihoods are presented. The vulnerability of farmer groups, including women, is


---
**Page 87**
---

acknowledged, especially with regard to their food insecurity as presented in the funding proposal.

28. Colombia is classified as an upper middle-income country. However, the proposal makes a case that the proposed interventions, while the cost of technology adoption, require concentration and scale make the project stable. As no country has the capacity to adopt the required technologies, the Government of Colombia is taking low concessional sovereign financing from GCF to support the implementation of the proposed technologies.

29. The proposal explains the needs of the recipient based on the absence of alternative financing although such justification is acknowledged, the justification could have been strengthened by providing a wider investment strategy, including a financial estimation of the cost of implementing the project in the national budget and the associated shortfalls in the national budget. Such an exercise would have provided a clear roadmap for how the project will be implemented within the sector from a financial perspective and would clarify the level of contribution from GCF.

3.5 Country ownership
Scale: High

30. The proposal demonstrates that the project is aligned with policies and strategies at both the national and the sectoral level, including with the country's nationally determined contributions. The proposal makes it clear which objective the proposed project will contribute to in its national context.

11. The project will be implemented by two executing entities (EEs): the Ministry of Finance and Public Credit (MFP) and the National Agricultural Research Institute (INAGRI). MFP will lead the project, while INAGRI will be responsible for technical implementation of the project, supported by CIAT, CGIAR, and other stakeholders. The project will include a management capacity assessment of the EEs and CIAT. MAIR and CIAT will track records of implementation in Colombia and other regions of the country, with comparable sizes of investments.

12. The proposal includes a detailed description of stakeholder consultations with relevant stakeholders, including the GCF national designated authority, civil society organizations, indigenous peoples, women, and other vulnerable groups, and all the stakeholders involved in this project. A stakeholder consultation report was submitted as part of the funding proposal (annex 7).

3.6 Efficiency and effectiveness
Scale: High

13. The project is highly efficient with a GCF cost of USD 1.45 per tCO2eq, which is assessed to be efficient compared with benchmarks of similar investments in similar regional contexts. The project will have an internal rate of return (IRR) of 80 percent and a net present value (NPV) of 1:36.

14. The proposed structure, with a mix of GCF grants and loans, is assessed to be adequate and reasonable. ADI developed nine financing mix scenarios resulting in various outcomes for the profitability of the activities considered for each producer organization. The scenarios consider different grant levels (from 0 percent to 75 percent) and loan interest rates (from reductions in grant amount of 35 and 75 per cent). The GCF and CAF loan mixture scenario shows that the project is profitable when the grant component is reduced by 35 percent, while value chains not profitable, while when the grant element is reduced by 75 percent, four out of eight value chains are not profitable. The model demonstrates that the grant component is more important for profitability than the loan component and that the loan component in the profitability of the interventions for each producer association.
