### Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

#### 4.1 Environmental and social safeguards

Project background: The project aims to develop, validate, and implement new low-emission and resilient technologies and to scale up existing technologies such as genetic improvement of rice varieties and the use of improved water management techniques. These actions are to be supported by the implementation and operation of climate information services that will be available to various stakeholders and decision-makers. Among the environmental and social co-benefits, the project is expected to contribute to reducing greenhouse gas emissions through carbon sequestration through implementation of various soil and water conservation strategies.

Among the risks associated with the project are adverse impacts as a result of reduced food supply, particularly during climate-induced natural events.

Environmental and social risk category and safeguard instruments: The project is classified in the environmental and social risk category as low. The project does not involve significant environmental or social risks and/or impacts which are site-specific, largely reversible and can be mitigated with appropriate measures. A detailed environmental and social risk assessment (ESR) and a social assessment is included in the ESS that has been prepared for the project. The Secretariat confirms that the project meets the criteria of environmental and social risk categorization and is within the risk accreditation level of AE.

Compliance with GCF environmental and social safeguards standards: The paragraph below outlines how the proposed project complies with the GCF environmental requirements:

ESS 1: Assessment and management of environmental, social risks and impacts. The project will be assessed as an ESMF which included an environmental and social risk assessment and management measures as provided in the included environmental and social management plan (ESMP). The ESMP includes a description of the project site, including its extent/location, permanence, duration, reversibility and significance of the risks/impacts and associated mitigation measures. A summary of the results of the environmental and social risk assessment are presented and an ESMP is proposed that identifies potential impact areas, required mitigation measures, and responsible parties for each of these things. The ESMP is built on the basis that the project will comply with applicable national laws, regulations and standards for the protection of the environment and communities as well as that of the host country. It also includes a description of any existing or anticipated conflicts, armed conflict as well as between beneficiary and non-beneficiary conflicts that may be generated by the implementation of this project. The risks associated with the implementation of the project – these will be mitigated at one of the site selection where the security and conflict situations are appropriately assessed, and low-risk areas will be prioritized and selected for project implementation.

ESS 2: Labour and working conditions (the workers that may be hired by the project (farmers and other stakeholders) will be hired in accordance with the terms of the current labour law in the country. Although farmers will be mostly beneficiaries, should farmers also be hired, the same terms and conditions will be applied. The project will also


---
**Page 89**
---

### GCF Project Document Page

#### ES3: Resource efficiency and pollution prevention
The project will focus on reducing waste and more efficient use of resources including chemicals and pesticides. Although health risks associated with the use of these inputs in the production process are recognized, the project will not include interventions that would require the displacement of people, either physically or economically.

#### ES4: Community health, safety and security
As indicated above, areas with security and conflict issues are excluded from this project. In line with the project’s approach to health and safety, each of the implementing partners will be required to have an occupational health and safety plan to manage the risks to people in the environment.

#### ES5: Biodiversity conservation and sustainable management of natural living resources
The project's activities under component 2 will promote management practices and other interventions that are consistent with national regulations regarding restrictions and the permit system. The AE also stated that attention will be paid to interventions (e.g., in potatoes and sweet potato chains) which will be carried out in areas where there is a prohibition or heavy penalties for heavy machinery in agricultural activities, such as final disposal, management of burning and solid/or hazardous waste, including composting, land preparation, harvesting, logging and fumigation and spraying of chemicals. The project will also explore relationships with communities to ensure that they have access to information relating to ecosystem services, biodiversity and sustainable practices that are beneficial for them.

#### ES6: Biodiversity conservation and sustainable management of natural living resources
The project will not have a direct impact on indigenous peoples and their territories. To encourage the inclusion of indigenous peoples in the project’s activities, a gender-differential approach is included in the baseline, risk assessment, and the mitigation measures, as well as in the disclosure and engagement processes to be implemented by the


---
**Page 90**
---

project. Specific measures to achieve this ethnic differential approach are: including people from ethnic communities in the project’s intervention areas and in the project’s technical team, especially in the group of extension workers; to build trust and facilitate work with the communities; providing training for the extension workers on gender equality issues in the information-gathering forms and the databases of the producer associations; and preparing a guidebook on gender equality that will be used by the extension workers when formulating policies of the producer associations participating in the project. If in the future the project directly involves indigenous peoples, the project will proceed in accordance with the GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy and domestic law.

### 4.2 Gender policy

The AIF has provided a gender assessment and gender action plan, and therefore complies with the requirements of the GCF Gender Policy.

The gender assessment provided illustrates the existence of an enabling environment that is conducive to the pursuit of gender equality in the project area. The Constitution ratified important international treaties and its Constitution guarantees equal rights and opportunities to women and men. The laws enacted dictate equal opportunities for women as


---
**Page 91**
---

## The Gender Assessment Provided by the AE Was Compiled on the Basis of Desk Reviews and Stakeholder Consultations with Female Producers and Associations

The assessment included interviews with women producers, extensionists, agricultural officials, extensionists, affiliated female and male producers, and institutional actors. The assessment indicates that women face a number of challenges in accessing resources and services. Their situation is exacerbated by the gender norms and relations in the household and on the farm. Compared to men, women have less access to land and financial and technological resources, and therefore are more vulnerable to food insecurity, particularly families with young children and households. Women spend more than men (and responsible for most of the unpaid care work, with women spending approximately 8 hours a day on such tasks), compared with 3 hours by men. This is due to the fact that women have less access to land and other resources to manage and cope with climate-induced vulnerabilities, and contribute to the deepening of food insecurity in rural areas.

### Further, in the Agriculture Sector, Women Producers Face Challenges in Accessing to Produce Assets Such as Land, Technical Assistance, and Credit

These challenges are seen in the value chain for crops such as rice, maize, beans, cassava, yams, and vegetables. In livestock and potato chains have the lowest proportion of production units headed by women (fewer than 10% of all units). The main reason is that women do not own land or livestock. This issue is not only that women lack access to land but it is also due of size they own or manage, which is very small compared with men. In the case of potatoes and cassava, significant differences are found mainly in the cattle chain, where it is only 41 per cent). Access to credit as an issue for women producers is a major challenge in the sector – regardless of crop – able to receive some sort of financing for production. Most of these challenges reflect the existing disparities in the participation and benefit of women. The associations lack capacity to provide technical assistance, and there are no specific programs or projects addressing the challenges faced by women in the sector.

### The Gender Action Plan

The gender action plan and therefore complies with the requirement of the GCF Gender Policy. The gender action plan contains activities, targets, indicators, budget, timelines (by way of schedule and annual budgets) and gender experts to support with the implementation of the gender action plan. The implementation of the gender action plan will also involve the Ministry of Agriculture’s Rural Women Empowerment Program (RWEPP), which is a program that has been raised in the assessment, thereby promoting gender equality in systems and institutions. It will ensure an increase in women’s access to land and other resources for production, improve forecasts in agriculture and that access to women producers for agricultural extension services is made available. The project will also work on ensuring that women have more opportunities as women with decision-making roles, thereby making women beneficiaries leaders in the process of implementing CSGAP’s plans and activities for the conservation, preservation and revitalization of natural resources, including the use of agroforestry systems to control management of pests and diseases, led by women farmers’ groups and/or organizations. To address the challenges faced by women producers, the project will provide training and will disseminate models for young rural women on primary processing, management and marketing of agricultural products, as well as access to credit. The project will also work to credit easier, ensuring women participate in farmers’ groups or organizations and ensuring women have more opportunities as women with decision-making roles.

### Project Implementation

The project will be implemented by the Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry, taking into account the importance and burden of care work on women. The project will work on ensuring the availability of local care services while at the same time raising awareness of gender issues and


---
**Page 92**
---

gender-based violence among both women and men. The latter could also be reported using the project-based grievance redress mechanism, which will focus on complaints from women and ethnic groups.

The activities included in the gender action plan are intended to empower women and the community at large by providing women with training, access to information on agricultural value chains, agronomic information, information and communication techniques, and mechanisms for accessing credit. The project also aims to create a more enabling environment for women to participate more in decision-making and sustainable practices on agriculture, thereby increasing their productivity, sphere of influence and capacity in the agricultural sector, as well as to increase their incomes.

## Risks

### Overall programme assessment (medium risk)

The objective of the project is to reduce the vulnerability of agricultural production to climate threats; to minimize the impacts of those threats on the competitiveness of the agricultural sector; and to contribute to the achievement of the EU’s climate change policy objectives, including reducing greenhouse gas emissions from agriculture. The GCF financing consists of (i) a sovereign loan to the Government of Colombia of up to USD 25 million; and (ii) a grant of up to USD 14 million. Both the loan and the grant will be provided by local producer associations totalling USD 16.3 million. Both the resources will be used for the same purpose as provided by the GCF, and the AE will be used by the EE to provide grants to the beneficiaries.

The project comprises three components: (i) digital agriculture and climate services for rural micro-enterprises; (ii) training of local farmers in sustainable agricultural practices, crop management techniques and other technological options and their scaling up including climate change adaptation and promotion low-carbon agricultural development; and (iii) innovative and inclusive business models through modernized innovation systems and a more engaged financial sector.

### Accredited entity/executing entity's capability to execute the current programme (medium risk)

The GCF is the AE for the project. GCF is rated AA by Standard & Poor’s and Fitch, CAF has a local presence in Colombia and nearly 11 per cent of CAF portfolio (at end year 2019) is located in Colombia. The Government of Colombia holds 53.8 per cent of CAF 23M shares in project value. The Government of Colombia is a shareholder of the AE.

The Government of Colombia will be the EE and will be the entity responsible for the project implementation, including the execution of the project activities and resources provided by the AE. MADR will be responsible for executing the project resources and technical implementation.

### Project-specific execution risks (medium risk)

Demand risk: The success of the project depends on the demand of local farmers for the new technologies. Low uptake could result in project delay or lower-than-expected impacts. This risk is partially mitigated by (i) involving farmer organizations; and (ii) the relatively high expected take-up providing financial incentives for uptake by farmers.

The TCA will be signed by CIAT and MADR. The project execution unit (MADR), on the other hand, will be executed by the Government of Colombia, through MADR and GCF.

### 4\.5 Results monitoring and reporting

73\. As a cross-cutting initiative, the programme aims at generating both mitigation and adaptation results. Mitigation results are expected to result in respect to overall GHG emission reduction of 8,152,034 tCO₂eq over the project lifespan of 15 years, as per the metrics of the GCF core indicator for mitigation (based on the GHG accounting methodology detailed under paragraph 76). The programme is expected to generate adaptation results in terms of 347,9996 indirect beneficiaries as expected adaptation impact. The AE provided a robust framework for the monitoring and evaluation using the indicators listed at page 24 of the funding proposal.

74\. The theory of change diagram captures current levels of expected changes as well as how these will be translated from the goal statement to the proposed activities. The barriers and risks that may hinder the achievement of these changes are identified and linked to pertinent monitoring and evaluation (M&E) indicators.

75\. The logical framework has been designed in accordance with relevant GCF-level impacts and outcomes as well with project-specific outputs. In most cases baselines and targets of each indicator have been established through a combination of secondary data, primary data, and national surveys.

76\. As it is welcome to have a baseline starting at the first year of project implementation as indicated in Annex 5 [Implementation timeline] of the funding proposal, through which the AE will establish concrete definitions and methodologies for its performance indicators. # Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP182

Proposal name: Climate-smart initiatives for climate change adaptation and sustainability in prioritized agricultural production systems in Colombia

Accredited entity: Corporación Andina de Fomento (CAF)

Country/[ies]: Colombia

Programme/programme size: Medium

## Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

### 1.1 Impact potential Scale: Medium to high

Concept: This is a semi-experimental funding proposal for scaling and upgrading the "digital agriculture" pilot initiative of the Government of Colombia. The proposal targets nine agriculture value chains (rice, maize, potatoes, sugarcane, palm cane, coffee, banana, plantains and livestock) in 20 departments and 219 municipalities across the country. It is a nationwide approach and will be implemented in 22 departments and 219 municipalities of Colombia, directly benefiting approximately 453,867 smallholder farmers (SHF), their families, and producer associations and about 424,820 people their family members. The funding proposal is expected to reduce or avoid 9.15 million t CO₂eq during the 25-year lifespan of the project (fiscal year 2023-2047).

The pilot phase of the digital agriculture initiative was initiated in 2012 by the Alliance for Innovation and Development of Agriculture (AIDAG) with support from the Global Alliance for Climate Smart Agriculture (GACAT) in cooperation with the Ministry of Agriculture and Rural Development (Ministerio de Agricultura y Desarrollo Rural [MADR]) of Colombia and was implemented in 2013-2015. It covered six pilot municipalities in three departments: Antioquia, Cauca, and Huila.

The capacities of two crop producer associations, known as genmos ("Federico" (male) and "Femenile" (female)), were strengthened through the pilot phase to provide technical assistance on success, barriers and gaps and supported development of this funding proposal. The pilot phase had four basic activities to mitigate climate change effects on Colombian farmers: (a) Agrometeorological risk management focuses on development and validation of climate variability and long-term predictions for agriculture sectors in different Colombian


---
**Page 98**
---

### Site-specific agriculture management includes identification problems that limit agricultural sector growth, collect information at the farm level, and propose technologies to improve productivity and reduce greenhouse gas emissions while increasing food security.

#### (b) Testing new agricultural technologies for adaptation to climate change hazards and challenges in the pilot phase. The project will test and evaluate new and traditional agricultural practices that contribute to reducing GHG emissions and offer alternative livelihoods for smallholder farmers, including the use of intercropping systems of palm trees, intensive silvopastoral systems and fruit trees was measured. Soil loss and the water footprint of potatoes were also measured, and cassava materials were evaluated.

#### (c) Evaluating sustainable production systems targeting the identification of sustainable productivity pathways for smallholder farmers in the pilot phase. The project will use new tools and technologies to mitigate climate change effects. In the pilot phase capacity building was provided to 1000 farmers on the use of digital agriculture services, areas information was delivered directly to producers. Targeted Grenada, Fenddoraa and Fenicea, respectively for rice and maize, have found great potential in the information system.

The independent Technical Advisory Panel (TAP) considers that the main result of this pilot is the development of a sustainable agricultural model that can be replicated by research centres, non-governmental organisations (NGOs), producer associations and farmers.

5. The funding proposal consists of three interconnected components each with two result areas identified as follows:

#### Component 1 covers deployment of digital agriculture and climate services for rural modernization with an emphasis on adaptation and mitigation. Component 1 activities will be implemented in the following pilot sites: Grenada, Fenddoraa, Fenicea, Humid Caribbean, Inter-Andean Valleys, Foreshore/Mid mountain, High Mountain, Eastern.

Result 1.1 is strengthened and modernised digital agricultural and extension systems with an emphasis on adaptation and mitigation. Seven "Big Data" platforms will be established to provide access to a wide range of data including weather, soil, processed information that considers users' social, ethnic and gender differences; production and market information; and climate change adaptation and mitigation measures to producers; management of agricultural risks allows provision of timely and reliable information to more than one million users (considered indirect beneficiaries) and 104,000 direct beneficiaries with access to recommendations to avoid losses.

Result 1.2 established and/or strengthened agricultural services to provide recommendations on adaptation and mitigation measures. Direct technical support is provided to approximately 123,589 producers and indirect support to 104,000 users

*Big Data Technologies can be defined as software tools for analyzing, processing, and extracting data from an extremely complex and large data set with which traditional management tools can never deal.


---
**Page 99**
---

and 13 Agricultural Technical Working Groups which will receive recommendations through the climatic services platforms on how to reduce crop losses.

### Component 2 considers improvements of genetic resources (tolerance to climate variability), crop management techniques and other technological options and their scaling up, as well as the development of new varieties and seed systems for climate development. Activities of component 2 will take place at CIAT's campus in Cali, Colombia, and at experimental stations located in six agricultural regions as component 1.

Result 2.1 is a strengthened germplasm bank; new climate resilient varieties developed, and their seeds massively distributed. A key deliverable is design and development of a state-of-the-art Phenotyping Platform to identify low-carbon production and climate-resilient crop genotypes (e.g., sorghum, maize, soybean, rice, wheat, beans).

Result 2.2 is improved knowledge on the water and carbon footprints of selected seed chains. In this regard development of new crop management techniques and technologies for climate change adaptation and mitigation will be supported. Climate resilience and GHG mitigation will be supported in the targeted value chains for different crops (e.g., sorghum, maize, soybean, rice, wheat, beans). New emission and resilient technologies should be developed, validated and implemented and 70 existing technologies that are climate-resilient and/or have low-carbon footprints (or other technological options) should be scaled up. At 131,710 hectares resilience to climate variability will be promoted, reducing 432,979 tonnes of GHG. These activities will mainly take place at CIAT's campus in Cali, Colombia, and at experimental stations located in the six agricultural regions (e.g., Coastal plain, Andean highlands, Valley, Foothills/Mid-mountain, High mountain, Eastern Plains) since they must be performed under different climatic conditions.

### Component 3 should ensure identification and implementation of innovative and inclusive business models through modernized innovation systems and a more engaged financial sector. The activities will take place at CIAT's campus in Cali, Colombia for the two components; component 2 will be implemented in all the previously prioritized locations.

Result 3.1 of this component is improved business models that provide farmers with reasonable profits and improved GHG mitigation practices. In particular, improved business models should be developed and implemented to ensure their operational and effective for at least 60 entities of the agricultural sector (including Departmental and Municipal Governments) in the six agricultural regions.

Result 3.2 should be modernized technical assistance and agricultural extension services with multi-tool strategies, adapted to specific environmental, social and gender aspects. In particular, these activities will provide technical assistance to more than 54,000 agricultural producers for low-carbon and resilient agriculture considering environmental, social and gender aspects.

In the case of rice and maize, the aim is to scale-up technologies that were previously evaluated in the pilot and were found to be effective. These include: (i) the use of organic fertilizers and biofertilizers in rice; Centracida (private), non-profit corporation, founded in 1977 at the initiative of the Association of Sugar Cane Growers of Colombia (ASACO). It is a producer of organic fertilizers and biofertilizers. The main environmental effect on fertilizers: Centracida with more efficient biotransfer systems and better retention. Fungicides, OPVAF, OPAF, and other agrochemicals are also used in rice production. (ii) the use of drip irrigation for the efficient use of fertilizers and harvesting; treatment and use of water in agroindustry models; in potatoes with minimum tillage and conservation agriculture developed by the GMT Alliance.

### 1.2 Paradigm shift potential Scale: High

- The independent TAP considers that the success of this approach, as described in the funding proposal, could significantly contribute to the future of sustainable and safe food security.
- According to the Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO), to produce a ton of wheat requires 1.8 tons of water, while producing a ton of beef requires 15 tons of water. Reducing agricultural GHG emissions and maintaining or reducing the land used in agriculture and "digital agriculture" could address the need for more efficient use of resources, including water.
- The proposal's vertical dissemination of climate information to farmers is expected to increase their resilience to climate change by allowing farmers to be more actively involved and to ensure an equal level of access to the digital common good. This will also help to better reflect the final impact of the initiative; the independent TAP scored this criteria medium to high.

2030 has already been met with the support of FAO and Consortium of International Agricultural Research Centers (CGIAR).

12. The Approach offered in the funding proposal is progressive and covers most of the gaps existing in the agriculture sector how to reduce the sector vulnerability including climate change variability and long-term changes in agricultural systems. The proposal has to ensure maximum digitalization of entire value chains for nine agricultural activities: (i) crop production; (ii) livestock production; (iii) aquaculture; (iv) agroforestry model climate change at local level; manage information with big Data Systems and Planting Information System; (v) post-harvest management; (vi) processing and transformation of germplasm; ensure horizontal and vertical dissemination of information (early warning process, innovative techniques, technologies and tools); ensure security of biodiversity, food and health related to the use of genetically modified organisms; ensure access to markets for smallholder business models; ensure financial institutions access to information to develop tailored financial products; and ensure that the project will be able to increase the number of farmers by 100 per cent capacity in each of the technologies proposed in the framework of the project.

The same time, the independent TAP recognizes the complexity of the activities planned at but the same time, the independent TAP considers that the funding proposal is progressive and covering real impact and effect, real transformational change. The independent TAP confirms that the role of this funding proposal will be to support the implementation of the CGIAR’s agriculture sector initiated by the pilot phase. In case of success, the approach could also be a model for other agricultural countries.

### 1.3 Sustainable development potential Scale: Medium to high

A comprehensive Environmental Sensitive Monitoring Framework (ESMF) is provided for this funding proposal in order to identify areas and components where each component are detailed. During the review Component was particularly targeted for assessment of risks and mitigation measures. The incorporation and enforcement of the risk mitigation measures will be a key element in ensuring that the project benefits the environment, communities and project beneficiaries. The project’s information disclosure strategy and governance structure will ensure transparency and accountability to all relevant stakeholders.

Component 2 of the funding proposal consists of activities related to the increase of crop resilience against climate variability and long-term changes in agricultural systems. Among these activities are genetic modification for development of improved seeds, hybrids and close resistant varieties; breeding and selection of new varieties; and the commercialization of these modified seeds in regions with regular conditions. Such activities could have some risks for food security and health related to the use of genetically modified organisms and require relevant control by the Government and through international treaties. Within this context, the independent TAP has reviewed the independent TAP in the project’s ESMF, which is annex 6 of the funding proposal package.

The ESMF document demonstrates that where research on genetic improvements of crops and the introduction of genetically improved organisms are carried out, there will be

# Compliance with Regulations

The ESFM considers all relevant regulations in force in this area including chapter 2 (Basic Genetic Materials of Improved Seeds) and chapter 3 (Living Modified Organisms) of the Soil Regulation No. 1071/2015, as well as the Law on Agricultural Development Administrative Sector, Decree 1071 of 2015.

### Article 2.1.3.7.2.2

The ICA (Instituto Colombiano de Agropecuaria y Desarrollo Rural) has the function of supervising the registration, certification, multiplication and distribution of all improved materials in Colombia.

### Article 2.1.3.7.2.3

All basic genetic materials produced by private industry that are intended for commercialization to the public, in the form of improved materials, must be registered with the ICA.

### Article 2.1.3.7.2.4

The ICA must certify seeds derived from improved material and their multiplication.

### Regulatory Framework for Living Modified Organisms (LMOs)

The regulatory framework for Living Modified Organisms (LMOs) following the provisions of Law 740/2002, which approves the "Cartagena Protocol on Biosafety" to the Convention on Biological Diversity, includes the registration, certification, multiplication and distribution of LMOs.

### Risk Management Measures

The ESFM considers risk management measures for Activity 2.1: Strengthening the genetic diversity of potato varieties in order to increase their adaptability and mitigate seed multiplication techniques will be applied to outstanding varieties that are currently under threat due to lack of adaptation or resistance to diseases.

In addition, attention will be paid to investments carried out in the framework of Law 1930/2018. This issue is of special attention for the potato value chain.


---
**Page 103**
---

# Mitigation measures planned:

(a) The project will comply with restrictions stated in the regulations, specifically in Law 1930 of 2018 article 5, which establishes prohibitions on some productive practices in montane areas: the use of fire for agricultural and pastoral activities; the final disposal, management and burning of solid and/or hazardous waste; the introduction or propagation of exotic species; burning logging; fumigation and spraying of chemicals should be gradually eliminated. The planting of trees, shrubs, grasses, and other native species; and the degradation of natural vegetation cover, among other actions, is prohibited.

(b) The project will not carry out activities within delimited montane areas (there are 37 montane areas in total). This measure is necessary to ensure that the scope of the project to provide assistance to producers located in those areas, the aforementioned prohibitions are respected. In addition, it will be ensured that the participation of those producers in training spaces that will inform about and promote the development of productive activities compatible with the conservation measures on these ecosystems. As mentioned above, these measures need special attention in the potato and livestock value chains.

(c) The technical assistance team (technical team), with support from the producer associations, will identify the male and female producers who carry out their productive activities within delimited montane areas. This measure is necessary to ensure that the implementation of project activities developing sustainable agriculture practices. They will provide guidance and technical assistance for producers in these areas to participate in training sessions on the conservation measures established by the scope of Law 1930 of 2018.

(d) The producer associations, CIAT, Agrovisa and MABR are responsible for monitoring activity 2.1 compliance with ESMF Key groups for monitoring are: producers who have their crops in areas close to delimited montane; cattle breeders who have their livestock production activities within the delimited montane areas; and producers who have their farms that implement sustainable production practices; producers in areas near delimited montanes that implement conservation measures.

Detailed performance criteria for monitoring are provided in the ESMF Key Groups Monitoring Scheme. The independent TAP takes into consideration that GDF does not have policy authority over genetically modified organisms (GMOs) in Colombia, but rather that this responsibility lies with the Ministry of Agriculture and Livestock (MAG). ### Needs of the recipient Scale: High

The feasibility study describes everyday challenges faced by Colombia's agriculture sector; by producers and grantees as price issues and competitiveness, some with international competitors. The agricultural sector is also facing other challenges that are exacerbated by climate change and weather-related losses. Colombia's internally nationally determined contributions (NDCs) for adaptation and mitigation of climate change in the agricultural sector include a commitment to the development of joint efforts between the different institutions of the private sector (greros), public sector (MAG), and research sector (CUC, INIA, CIP, IICA, etc.). The feasibility study proposes ten agricultural subsectors such as rice, coffee, livestock and silvopastoral systems, with improved productivity and competitiveness as the main objectives of the project.

There is a clear commitment in the agricultural sector to aim at the objectives proposed in the nationally determined contributions (NDCs) for adaptation and mitigation of climate change in the agricultural sector. The feasibility study proposes ten agricultural subsectors such as rice, coffee, livestock and silvopastoral systems, with improved productivity and competitiveness as the main objectives of the project.

The digitalization of agriculture should be considered as the future of this sector worldwide. Colombia has experienced a significant drop in its agricultural GDP share since 1976. A drop in the share of national income in recent decades, from 22 per cent of GDP in the late 1970s to 6.4 per cent in 2018. However, the sector shows a slight recovery, with growth of 2.4 per cent and 2.3 per cent for 2018 and 2019 respectively. Areas identified as exhibiting slower development of the sector are:

The Nagoya Protocol is significant in terms of conservation of biodiversity, and for having an equitable sharing of benefits arising from genetic resources. It also aims to help indigenous peoples everywhere to avoid being exploited for their traditional knowledge and expertise.

### 1. Country ownership

#### High

As was already highlighted above, this funding proposal is spreading the national programme for climate-smart agriculture of Colombia represented by the MAID in partnership with the Alliance of Bioversity International and CIAT via an agreement for technical and scientific cooperation (TAC) signed on March 2019. The expected direct and indirect effects on Colombian farmers are detailed under the investment criteria "impact assessment".

The funding proposal has two executing entities: MAID and the International Center for Tropical Agriculture (CIAT). Both entities will be responsible for project implementation and supervising CIAT to ensure that all activities are executed on time and with the required quality.

The Government of Colombia through the Ministry of Finance and Public Credit will be the entity responsible for project resources, both credit and grant resources from GCF, as well as CGI. The Ministry of Finance and Public Credit will also be in charge of coordinating technical implementation through its Directorate of Innovation, Technological Development

# Health Protection

The Government of Colombia will take loans from both GCF and CAF and will provide grants to the beneficiaries (gremios) given the public goods nature of the interventions. The MAIR will be supported in the operation of this project by the Alliance of Biodiversity Trusts (ABT). This alliance is a non-profit organization that provides technical assistance to the legislation and regulations of the Government of Colombia. In addition, CAF will develop a model for the implementation of the project, which will include a monitoring plan including the details of the activities, the co-financing provided by each gremio, and at the end of the project, a report on the results achieved.

The project will have three strategic local partners for project implementation, of which there are ten gremios and three research institutions. For the execution of the CICAP project (CICAP Colombia), the following partners will be involved: Cooperativa de Trabajo Femenino (Matrén), Federación (Derechit), Fedepaís (pandora cacao), Fedepazas (potato), Federación Nacional de Cafeteros - Cenicafe (coffee), Acocola - Cecafutla (agriculture), Aguirra, Ashoma, and Andromeda. The project will also have three strategic implementing partners: the Colombian Agricultural Research Corporation (Agrosavia), the International Center for Tropical Agriculture (CIAT), and the Colombia Institute of Research on Sustainable Agricultural Production Systems (CIPAV) de Colombia. These strategic partners will be responsible for the implementation of the project, which has been designed with a budget that will be subject to outputs and deliverables. The importance of these partner institutions is that they are responsible for the continuity and sustainability of the project actions once the project is completed.

The project has undergone a review by the Collegiate Body (made up of the Ministry of Environment and Sustainable Development, the National Planning Department, the Ministry of Foreign Affairs, the Presidential Agency for International Cooperation) which supported the proposal. The current version of the funding proposal has incorporated the comments provided by the Collegiate Body. This process has verified the alignment of the project to the goals of the National Development Plan 2018-2022, the country's commitments under the Paris Agreement, and the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), as well as the objectives of the National Strategy for Food Security and Poverty Eradication.

According to the recently updated NDC on Colombia’s adaptation priorities for agriculture is improvement of capacities and information access to prioritized agricultural interventions. The project will contribute to this goal by ensuring that 50 percent of programmed actions for risk management, climate change adaptation, and food security are implemented in the context of food security and poverty eradication so as to ensure that 50 per cent of all programmed actions for risk management, climate change adaptation, and food security are implemented.

The funding proposal directly contributes to the achievement of the goals of the NDCs, which are to strengthen the capacity of agricultural producers through the creation of a national Spanish Agrometric Technical Work Groups at 27 departments, reaching one million users. The proposed project is fully aligned with the relevant country strategies and policies. It is a part of the National Strategy for Food Security and Poverty Eradication.

The exit strategy described in the funding proposal highlights country ownership through demonstrating clear roles for different stakeholders. The funding proposal informs that at the end of the project, the data platform will be delivered to the Ministry of Agriculture (to remain in the hands of MAGR) but once the project is completed, they will be delivered to the gremios through a contract with the Colombian Agricultural Research Corporation (Agrosavia) (including data platforms) but undertake to give it proper use and assume maintenance costs.

# Efficiency and effectiveness: Scale: Medium to high

## Key areas identified by the independent TAP

1. **Funding Proposals:** The TAP considered the funding proposals for the pilot project.
2. **Monitoring of Digital Agriculture Results:** The TAP monitored the results through well-developed performance indicators.
3. **Success in Achieving Success:** The TAP noted that the review process was successful, with weak horizontal penetration of information, knowledge, etc., which was not a problem because it was addressed by the project developers.

## Monitoring and Evaluation

The AE response on the issue of monitoring and evaluation of the pilot project was that it would be done through the independent TAP. However, at the time of the review, the progress report of the project was not available and consequently the Government of Colombia, the gremios and the producers were not able to provide any information.

## Initial Monitoring

According to the funding proposal, initial monitoring is the responsibility of gremios but gremios should get information/data from producers and it should be very clear in the agreement that the AE will monitor the project. The AE provided a revised version of the agreement which was signed by the gremios and a last-mile agreement between gremios and producers which were not in full satisfaction of the independent TAP. Later on, the AE provided revisely revised template agreements.

## Digital Communication

Regarding the digital communication capacity in targeted regions, additional information is needed to determine whether the infrastructure is adequate. Colombia has 136 per cent mobile phone penetration and 77 per cent broadband penetration, though adoption and use are skewed toward urban centres. Some 25 per cent of people (72 per cent of those with a smartphone) in rural areas have access to the internet at home. The digital divide is that only 16 per cent of rural households have interest in accessing the internet.

## Rural Digital Zones

About 80 per cent of the intervention municipalities have (or are in the process of implementing) "Rural Digital Zones" implemented by the Ministry of Information, Technologies and Communication (MITIC). In 2017, these zones provided access to the internet for up to 4 hours a day in areas with the highest concentration of the populated centres of municipalities. Accessible public spaces include schools, libraries, community centres, etc., which provide free access internet outside their home, including at interest cafes, education centres, and public hotspots. Furthermore, the proportion of the rural population with at least a feature phone is far larger


---
**Page 108**
---

that the operation with a smartphone. In addition, CQCAP will work with a multi-pronged strategy using a variety of channels including voice/call (e.g., interactive voice response [IVR]), short messaging system (SMS), radio/TV, videos, photo, text messages, chat/bots, Web applications, and social media (TV, radio, SMS, etc.). CQCAP will also use the GCF's CAT counts on a data centre with the capacity to attend to most of the project demands. In addition, CAT has access to BIDS (the higher grid computing centres) available through the RENATe academic network.

From the AIP review meeting and the review the implementation TAP understands that implementing the system and asynchronous communication tools was essential; an interim measure. This will allow the project in the field to synthesize or upload the information collected from the field. The TAP also recommends that, if possible, this kind of off-line communication might not be effective, and additional measures should be considered (e.g., a dedicated server) for the collection of data and its transmission directly to the final customers/producers who could significantly increase the final efficiency of project.

The strength of the funding proposal is partnership. The funding proposal and feasibility study highlight that the project will be implemented by a consortium of public and private institutions working towards a common goal. The importance of these partners lies in the fact that they have the necessary experience, skills, and resources to ensure the success of the project activities once the project is completed. This should guarantee the high efficiency and effectiveness of this project.

The independent TAP recognises all the challenges of the project and scores the effectiveness and efficiency potential of the funding proposal as medium to high.


### Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

The independent TAP recommends this funding proposal for approval by the GCF Board and recommends that the Secretariat establish a special monitoring regime for the implementation of ESMP commitments and establish periodic reporting to the Board on this issue.

Climate-smart initiatives for climate change adaptation and sustainability in prioritized agricultural production systems in Colombia

### Impact potential
CISCAP considers from its formulation the direct work and massive involvement of producers as a response to the lessons learned from the pilot. Components 1 and 2 of the CISCAP project consider massive implementation of technologies on producers' farms. CISCAP is including a whole community approach, which will be based on the massive implementation and dissemination of technologies. The producers' associations/ gremios and Agrasavia will allocate personnel and resources to ensure that the project activities are carried out in an efficient manner.

### Paradigm shift potential
No comments

### Sustainable development potential
CISCAP will not use genetically modified organisms. The Project (activities component 2) will use only conventional genetic improvement techniques. The two components of genetic improvement are divided into "conventional" and "genetic modified (GM)", both used to produce a new genotype with the same phenotype as the original one, but with an added trait such as adding new genes from other or the same species (transgenic, cigenics), inducing recombination within the genome by means of a mechanism. The conventional genetic improvement is the development of cultivars based on the mode of reproduction of the species (cross pollination, self-pollination or clonally propagation).

### Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

As mentioned before in the action of "Sustainable Development plan/plan", the CISCAP project (initiative and component 2) will not work with genetically modified organisms and will only use conventional methods of genetic improvement. In this sense, the ITAP recommendation about "The Board should consider revising the current procedure for the approval of ESFM commitments and establish periodic reporting to the Board" is based on the premise of the use of genetically modified organisms, which is not correct. We respectfully suggest that the recommendation should be reconsidered.

# Introduction

The following gender assessment is an input for the formulation of the project "Low Emission and Climate Resilient Agriculture" (LECARA), for its acronym in English). This Project seeks to improve agricultural productivity and resilience against climate change (maize, sorghum, beans, cassava, panels and sugarcane; and coffee) to mitigate their respective greenhouse gas emissions and adapt to climate change by increasing efficiency in the use of the natural resources and production.

In recent years, the agricultural activities associated with these eight products have been affected by variability in temperature and rainfall, with more frequent and intense floods, droughts, and frosts, which have sometimes led to crop losses. Besides, some agricultural and livestock practices have contributed to soil degradation. As a result, productive cycles have been altered and the productivity of these activities has decreased, affecting the sustainability of production. This in turn increases the economic vulnerability of male and female producers and puts food availability at risk.

These events may have differential repercussions depending on the characteristics of the territory (rural or urban), the sexes (men and women; or ethnic groups), as well as the type of producers (small, medium, or large), due to the unequal access to productive factors, information technologies and the mechanisms for adapting to climate change. In the case of rural areas, these differences are also related to the gender division of labor within the household and on the farm. The low participation in production decision-making, low access to and control over land and financial and technological resources, overcrowded domestic and unpaid work (which is more common among women), as well as the lack of autonomy (in terms of household), among other factors, limit the women's capacity to develop adaptation strategies in the face of climate variability. Besides, the effects on household food security may deepen because, on one hand, the responsibility for food supply falls on them; and, on the other hand, climate change may decrease the availability of water, food and resources for self-consumption.

Specifically, in rural areas of the country, where 5.8 million men and 5.2 million women live, there are differences in the socio-demographic conditions of the population. According to data figures from the National Institute of Statistics (INE), the percentage of young people aged between 10 and 19 years old is higher among women than men: 34% for women versus 28%. In terms of gender (in the working ages, between 20 and 59 years, the percentages are similar, at 67%, in both cases). Among young people aged under 10 years old, participation is also higher among women than men: 34% for women versus 28%. In addition, the older age group (above 60) shows a greater percentage of men. Women's participation in the labor market evidence significant gender gaps: The 5.8% of rural men are employed, compared to 36.8% of women. In addition, the rural female unemployment rate is higher than that of male workers: 17.9% versus 10%. These figures show greater restrictions faced by women in developing activities included in the System of National Accounts. This situation results in a lower capacity of rural women to generate income and, therefore, in a higher percentage of rural households living below poverty line (35.6%) of male-headed households; while this figure is 27.9% of female-headed households.

These gender gaps evidence structural causes that limit the equitable development of rural women and men and their communities. On the one hand, according to the National Time Use


---
**Page 114**
---

Survey: ENUF (2010-2013): women perform most of the unpaid work in rural areas; they spend approximately 8 hours a day on these tasks, compared to 3 hours spent by men. On the other hand, according to the 2014 National Agricultural Census, only 38% of the Agricultural Production Units in the country do women participate in decision-making on production. Furthermore, it is estimated that 75% of rural women producers have less access to productive assets such as land, technical assistance, and credit.

Within this framework, the main objective of the Gender Assessment is to provide a baseline for recognizing the initial social, economic, and environmental conditions of the CISCAP Project, with emphasis on identifying social and gender gaps in male and female producers in the prioritized territories. The assessment also aims at understanding why these gender-based approaches are considered in the formulation of the project, so that CISCAP not only avoids deepening of the gaps identified but also contributes to the transformation of the causes that generate them.

This Gender Assessment was obtained from mixed research methods and triangulation of secondary information (on norms and policies) and statistical information, mainly instruments (census and surveys) of the National Administrative Department of Statistics (DANE), as well as through a participatory process completed by the associations of an institutional capacity analysis form, as well as the development of individual and group sessions with association officials, extensionists, affiliated female and male producers, and institutional actors.

As a result, the Gender Assessment makes a comprehensive diagnosis with a gender perspective of the territories, population, and productive chains targeted by the CISCAP Project, as well as the institutions’ regulatory and policy frameworks. To this end, the document is organized into four chapters.

The first chapter presents a general diagnosis of the social and gender conditions of the territories prioritized by the CISCAP Project, which includes demographic age, and household headedness data; access to productive assets such as land, seeds, tools, inputs, credit, education, labor market, income, and use of time in paid and domestic work and unpaid care activities.

The second chapter characterizes the prioritized production chains, identifies gender gaps, and comprehensively analyzes different aspects that determine the success of including a gender approach in a climate-resilient agriculture program (Beuscher & Badtue, 2013); the social conditions of male and female producers, and their access to and control over productive assets. Taking into account this chapter analyzes the institutional capacities of the respective associations to manage gender issues.

The fourth and last chapter defines the Institutional and gender policy framework, which refers to the main international norms adopted by Colombia, as well as those established at the national level for the promotion of equity for women, especially those living in rural areas. Similarly, it mentions the main policies and programs in force that have been formulated and

# Population characterization with a gender perspective

According to DANE figures (2002a), the territories where the CISCAP Project will be implemented have a rural population of 2,99 million people, of which 53.8% (1.55 million) are men and 46.2% (1.44 million) women.

The following table shows the distribution by age groups. Graph 0.1 shows a progressive behavior in the population pyramid of the rural inhabitants of the Project municipalities, as well as that of the country's country as a whole. This is related to the fact that there are more young people than older ones and that there has been a relatively homogeneous reduction for each of the age groups. Additionally, when comparing the composition of the CISCAP municipalities regarding the national data, there is lower participation among those between 20 and 49 years old (which is higher in the rural situation) and higher participation those over 60 years of age. However, given that the decrease is relatively homogeneous—among each of the groups—in these municipalities it is not as pronounced as in the national context.

Regarding the participation of men and women in decision-making within households, of the 941,5 thousand households in the CISCAP municipalities, 7 out of 10 are headed by men, which is higher than that found in rural and urban areas (68% [graph 0.2]). Further, only 20.6%


---
**Page 116**
---

of female-headed households live with a spouse, while 31.9% of female heads of household are single, 24.9% are widowed, and 24.1% are separated (common-law or married). This reflects that in most of these households, women are the head of household, which increases the household's risk of falling into poverty since women earn less income than men to support their dependents.

Graph G-03: Sex of the head of household, Rural areas. CSCAP municipalities and rural national. 2018

| Municipios LEORa | 64.8% | 35.2% |
| --- | --- | --- |
| National | 69.9% | 31.1% |

Source: Prepared by the author based on (DANE) 2018

Now, from Social Census 150 of 2012, the Multidimensional Poverty Index was defined to establish a poverty index that measures dimensions of household poverty, considering simultaneous deficiencies in the dimensions of educational conditions of the household*, conditions of children and youth*, work*, health* and household services and housing* conditions.

In view of the above, and based on (DANE) 2018, it is identified that, of the 10,138 million rural people in the CSCAP departments, 9.47 million are in a condition of multidimensional poverty*, corresponding to 33.3%.* Figure lower than the national situation (56.5%). To capture territorial differences and identify municipalities with greater vulnerability, the Ministry of Social Inclusion used the Population and Housing Census. It shows that 47.3% of the municipalities prioritized by the CSCAP project have multidimensional poverty incidence rates between 40% and 60% and the overall average is 51%. The municipality with a higher incidence rate (above 80%) is La Unión, and the municipality with the lowest incidence rate (below 20%) is San José de Uré. In terms of poverty conditions, 37.4% of municipalities are in a condition of extreme poverty (above 60%) and not part of the project, and the average adjusted rural MPI for the rest of the municipalities is estimated at 54.0%. Therefore, in terms of poverty conditions, the prioritized municipalities are in a better situation compared with the rest of the country's rural territory.

*The educational conditions of the household are analyzed in terms of low educational achievement and illiteracy.
*The conditions of children and youth are analyzed based on school absenteeism, school lag, barriers to access to early childhood care services, and lack of education.
*Labor conditions are analyzed based on low unemployment and informal employment.
*Health conditions are analyzed through the presence of chronic diseases in adults and children, inadequate health service coverage, and poor access to essential medicines.
*Household services and housing conditions are analyzed considering the presence of basic water sources, inadequate waste disposal, inadequate floors, inadequate external walls, and critical overcrowding.

*A CSCAP department is one that has at least two municipalities to intervene in the program.

Regarding poverty status according to the gender of the head of household, graph 0.3 shows that 34.6% of the rural male-headed households are considered multidimensionally poor, compared with only 18.5% female-headed households. The main causes for male-headed and female-headed households correspond to informal work (91.9%), low educational attainment (75.5%), limited access to an improved water source (38.5%), and school lag (32.9%). However, when compared to male-headed households, female-headed households have a higher rate of active unemployment (64.0%) for male-headed households and 21.8% for female-headed households, which reflects the restrictions on women's entry into the labor market.

Additionally, there are large differences between the incomes earned by men and women in the agricultural sector. Specifically, in the agriculture, livestock, and hunting subsector, men's average income corresponds to 1 smml (salary minimum monthly legal), while women earn an average of 0.35 smmls (in Spanish), which is 33.5% of 1 smml. When analyzing these data according to the occupational position, it is identified that employees earn the highest labor income in the sector (48.2% for men and 67.9% for women); followed by self-employed workers (for men and 58.2% for women), day laborers (19% for men and 64% for women), and, finally, self-employed workers, who earn the lowest income in the sector (31% of 1 smml for men and 34% for women)

However, identifying the unemployment rate shows that while men have an unemployment rate of 3.2% in the CICAP departments, the rate for women is 9.9% (similar to the national rate). Therefore, the labor market in rural areas does not manage to absorb the supply of women's jobs. In this context, it should be noted that women are mainly involved in agricultural and livestock activities predominate, in which male paid labor prevails. Nevertheless, rural women participate in these tasks as unpaid family workers and care economy activities closely related to agriculture such as seed selection, conservation, transformation, and supply of food for self-consumption and day laborers, and animal care

In this sense, and thanks to Law 44/13 of 2013, the importance of unpaid work performed at home that is not usually economic (housing maintenance, care of household or community members, and maintenance of the paid labor force) is recognized, and the role of rural women in the economic and social development of their communities is becoming to be evident. In this regard, it should be noted that 65% of women perform activities included in the National Accounts (SNA) with a daily destination of 4:50 hours, and 92.5% carry out activities not included in the SNA for 07:52 hours. On the other hand, 65.5% of men perform SNA activities (8:25 hours) and 56.3% do so with a daily destination of 14:09 hours; women spend more time on domestic work per day than men (12:42 hours vs 11:31 hours, respectively), but approximately two-thirds of their time is spent on domestic and unpaid care activities, while men spend less than one-third of their own time in these areas.

Among the work activities not included in the System of National Accounts are: i) providing food to household members; ii) maintaining clothing for household members; iii) cleaning, maintenance, and repairs for the household; iv) household purchases and administration; v) care activities performed by women under 5 years of age belonging to the household; vi) support for household members in a passive care (being on standby). The gender gaps in each of these areas are presented below.

First, concerning to providing food to members of the household: (preparing and serving food; lifting and carrying dishes at home; preparing canned food, cheese or sausages; or taking food to people at their place of residence) women perform this activity more frequently than men. In all countries except Colombia, 23.9% of women perform these activities compared to 81.3% of women (graph 0.7). The regions with the greatest differences are the Atlantic region (17.4% of men and 82.5% of women) and the Central region (19.3% of men and 86.8% of women). On the other hand, the Eastern and Pacific regions show higher participation of men (20.1% and 27.1%, respectively).

Finally, taking care of other members of the household is an activity carried out mainly by women (57.4%) (Graph G.19). The gender gap is similar for the Atlantic and Central regions, where 38% of men take part in this activity. In the Pacific region is the one with the highest participation rates (19.4% of men and 44.2% of women).

## Characterization of production chains from a gender perspective

This chapter analyzes the production conditions, from a gender and ethnic perspective, in each of the prioritized chains: rice, bananas, coffee, paíra cacao, sugarcane, cattle, corn, and potatoes.

In general, issues and problems, both general and specific to each production chain, are summarized in Table 2.1 and must be considered in the General Action Plan and the Project's Environmental Management Manual, following the guidelines dictated by the regulations and policies on equity for women described in the following chapter.

Earned income

| --- | --- |
| Women | Average income for the agriculture, livestock and hunting sector: 13.9% (employee), employer (22%), the day laborer (90%) of the country and self-employed (17%). The average income for the agricultural production units is 58.6% of the current legal monthly minimum wage |

| Men | Average income for the agriculture, livestock and hunting sector: 34% of the current legal monthly minimum wage |
| Women | Average income for the agriculture, livestock and hunting sector: 104% of the country's average income (98% of the country's salary), of the country's corn, rice, livestock and potato chains have the lowest proportion of production units headed by women (less than 20%) and, in the case of bananas, the figure is more than 35%. |

Similarly, in terms of access to land, the size of production units in the municipalities prioritized for the CISCAP project is higher than the national average. However, the analysis revealed gender gaps not only about land ownership but also concerning zone, where on average the size of the production units headed by women is 10% smaller than that of men (the greatest differences are found mainly in the cattle chain, where it is only 43%).

Production factors, such as access to technical assistance and credit, condition access to other production factors. This is the case, for example, of potato producers who receive technical assistance, of whom 50% have access to credit, while among those who do not receive technical assistance, this percentage drops to only 13%. Among the producers receiving assistance in the rice and sugarcane chains, this percentage is between 20% and 30%, while in the banana, coffee, and sugarcane chains, it is between 50% and 60%. In addition, the analysis of the CISCAP project shows that there are differences in the topics taught through this service; among the topics taught through this service, there are some differences in behavior according to the sex of the producers. This reflects the importance of working in a participatory way with the different actors involved in the CISCAP project and the need for a more gender-sensitive approach.

Regarding the access to credit, less than 20% regardless of the crop were able to receive some method of financing for production. Besides, except for bananas and livestock, the gender gap in access to credit persists between male and female producers on the other chain.


Therefore, the figures show that, in general, there is a greater access to production factors by female producers than male decision makers on their own. This situation has repercussions on rural women's ability to generate income. Indeed, not only is the income received by farmers and ranchers lower for women (58% of men), but also the amount earned per hour worked is also quite different: while men earn, on average, 74.5% of the current legal monthly minimum wage, in the case of women it is no more than 33.5%. Furthermore, the data show that the income gap is present in all occupations (employee, employer, day laborer, and self-employed).


---
**Page 130**
---

This situation, added to the greater vulnerability and poverty levels of rural women who are heads of households (35.0% of female-headed households are in monetary poverty and 28.4% in multidimensional poverty, and 27.9% of male-headed households are in monetary poverty, and 28.9% in multidimensional poverty), makes this population group one that requires special attention to help close gender gaps.

Concerning ethnic groups, women who have a presence in the prioritized municipalities are indigenous. It was also identified that the participation of women and mixed producers tends to be higher in ethnic groups, especially in indigenous groups. To access productive factors and management practices of productive systems, wide gaps were identified according to ethnicity. In addition, it is important to note that there are no specific policies or programs for agroforestry production and conservation practices that are culturally rooted, several of which should be recognized and incorporated into the CSCAP project to generate trust and at the same time promote social inclusion in the productive process.

It is also important to note the demographic pressure on the Colombian countryside, which is not foreign to the municipalities where the CSCAP project will be implemented. In all the chains studied, there are women who are over 60 years old (see Table 2.1). This imposes challenges, not only in terms of promoting change, adopting knowledge and new technologies, and the communication and implementation methods used with these users.

### Capacities of the Producer Associations to manage gender issues

About the capacities of the nine Producer Associations participating in the CSCAP Project to manage gender issues, most of the entities do not have institutionalized channels, instruments, and resources to promote gender equality; however, some practices and projects developed with rural women are being implemented. The project aims at strengthening the capacities of these entities to guarantee the implementation of the Gender


---
**Page 131**
---

Action Plan of the CISCAP Project, as well as the sustainability of its results in the long term. The following are the main conclusions regarding the institutional capacities of each guild.

Producer Associations such as Fendace, Asocafed, Fedepapa, and Federaco have not implemented policies that do not have a gender action plan. This is due to the functioning of the strategic plans and follow-up tools without this guideline allowing observing the differentiated needs of both women and producers belonging to ethnic communities. However, despite not having a gender action plan, these producer associations have been able to include women within the guild, and 40% of its extensions are women. Concerning this, Fedepapa is the only union with these figures. On the other hand, Fendace and Asocafed, despite having salary equity policies, do not have a gender action plan that allows them to address the needs of ethnic producers to agricultural extension activities, and therefore the efforts made to establish inter-institutional links to promote programs or projects that benefit female members are not included in these plans. This is due to the lack of a framework on equity and gender, which is the recurrence of this type of projects. In other words, the lack of a general framework on equity and gender does not guarantee the sustainability of these actions or their harmonization with objectives.

Regarding the inclusion of the differential approach, Producer Associations such as Fendace and Fedepapa have not established policies to promote programs, projects, or policies to encourage the participation of ethnic communities belonging to their respective production chains. This is shown in the fact that they do not have a gender action plan, nor do they have specific actions, and the absence of areas whose function is to address issues related to the differential and gender approach. It may be that many of these affirmative actions are seen as a form of discrimination against women or ethnic producers within their respective production chains; the idea is that "equity" means providing all members with the same opportunities, without recognizing the different difficulties that vulnerable populations may have in accessing them.

On the other hand, Producer Associations with impact in regions with a high presence of ethnic communities have been able to include women and ethnic producers in the value processes of the guild, such as Fedepapa, which has carried out activities or projects aimed at improving the conditions of ethnic producers in this chain. It has been possible because they have established specific policies for these producers since they have membership forms that specify different aspects of the producers, which allow for a stated record of the associated population and their production conditions.

Equally, unions such as Fendace, Asocafed, Fedepapa, and Federaco scored below 40% in the financial capacity assessment with a gender perspective. This may be due to the lack of a general policy on equity and gender, which is necessary to access the budgetary markets associated with the affirmative actions that are carried out. The involvement of women in production processes requires not only differentiated technical assistance but also access to credit and other resources. In this sense, it would be important for the guild as a commitment from the guild to women and supporting their projects within the chain. In addition to the monitoring, follow-up, and evaluation component, these associations do not have information systems that allow them to know the number of women members or the number of women members. The lack of disaggregated information on the people working in
